Ascend Tutorial                               *verl-ascend_tutorial-en-README*

INTRODUCTION                          *ascend_tutorial-en-README-introduction*

Ascend fully supports the usage and development of verl. This documentation
provides a comprehensive guide on how to use verl on Huawei Ascend NPUs.

Last updated: 05/14/2026.

DIRECTORY STRUCTURE            *ascend_tutorial-en-README-directory-structure*

>text
    en/
    ├── get_start/                     # Quick start guide
    ├── feature_support/               # Feature support descriptions
    ├── model_support/                 # Model support descriptions
    ├── dev_guide/                     # Developer guide
    ├── faq/                           # Frequently asked questions
    └── contribution_guide/            # Community contribution guide
<

Model Evaluation                         *verl-dev_guide-model_dev-evaluation*

Last updated: 07/14/2026.

The steps are the same for different models; Qwen3-30B is used here as an
example.

We evaluate the model through AISBench, which supports evaluation with
multiple inference backends such as vllm/sglang.

1. INSTALLATION                  *dev_guide-model_dev-evaluation-installation*

>bash
    git clone https://gitee.com/aisbench/benchmark.git
    cd benchmark
    pip install -e .
<

              *dev_guide-model_dev-evaluation-download-the-evaluation-dataset*
2. DOWNLOAD THE EVALUATION DATASET

>bash
    cd path/to/benchmark/ais_bench/datasets
    wget https://opencompass.oss-cn-shanghai.aliyuncs.com/datasets/data/math.zip
    unzip math.zip
    rm math.zip
<

3. WEIGHT CONVERSION        *dev_guide-model_dev-evaluation-weight-conversion*

verl now supports saving HF-format model weights directly using mbridge, so
no conversion is needed.

If the model weights are not in HF format, convert them to HF format first
before evaluation.

Refer to verl's native |verl-advance-checkpoint|.

                    *dev_guide-model_dev-evaluation-vllm-inference-evaluation*
4. VLLM INFERENCE EVALUATION

Start the vllm_server service

Launch the inference server with the following command. Parameters to
modify: model and tensor-parallel-size.

model: the path to the converted HuggingFace model weights saved after
training;

tensor-parallel-size: the number of tensor-parallel replicas. TP is
recommended to match the infer configuration used during training;

data-parallel-size: the number of data-parallel replicas. DP is recommended
to match the infer configuration used during training, default is 1;

port: any free port can be set;

>bash
    vllm serve /path/to/Qwen3-30B/ \
           --served-model-name auto \
           --gpu-memory-utilization 0.9 \
           --max-num-seqs 24 \
           --max-model-len 22528 \
           --max-num-batched-tokens 22528 \
           --enforce-eager \
           --trust-remote-code \
           --distributed_executor_backend=mp \
           --tensor-parallel-size 8 \
           --data-parallel-size 1 \
           --generation-config vllm \
           --port 8080
<
Modify the AISBench inference configuration and launch the vllm_client
evaluation

Open the inference configuration file
benchmark/ais_bench/benchmark/configs/models/vllm_api/vllm_api_stream_chat.py

host_port must match the server's port; modify max_seq_len and max_out_len
according to the model configuration.

>python
    from ais_bench.benchmark.models import VLLMCustomAPIChatStream
    from ais_bench.benchmark.utils.model_postprocessors import extract_non_reasoning_content

    models = [
        dict(
            attr="service",
            type=VLLMCustomAPIChatStream,
            abbr='vllm-api-stream-chat',
            path="",
            model="",
            request_rate = 0,
            retry = 2,
            host_ip = "localhost",
            host_port = 8080,
            max_out_len = 512,
            batch_size=1,
            trust_remote_code=False,
            generation_kwargs = dict(
                temperature = 0.5,
                top_k = 10,
                top_p = 0.95,
                seed = None,
                repetition_penalty = 1.03,
            ),
            pred_postprocessor=dict(type=extract_non_reasoning_content)
        )
    ]
<
Open another terminal to run the evaluation, and launch the evaluation
command:

>bash
        ais_bench --models vllm_api_stream_chat --datasets math500_gen_0_shot_cot_chat_prompt
<

                  *dev_guide-model_dev-evaluation-sglang-inference-evaluation*
5. SGLANG INFERENCE EVALUATION

Refer to the evaluation section in
|verl-model_support-examples-ascend_sglang_best_practices|.

                              *verl-dev_guide-model_dev-parameter_and_metrics*
Training Configuration Parameters and Metrics

Last updated: 11/02/2026.

For NPU-related features, visit:
|verl-en-feature_support-npu_advance_features|.

verl manages all parameters through a hierarchical YAML configuration file.
All involved configuration files are located under the `verl/trainer/config`
directory.

 * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * *

*dev_guide-model_dev-parameter_and_metrics-configuration-parameter-descriptions*
1. CONFIGURATION PARAMETER DESCRIPTIONS

   *dev_guide-model_dev-parameter_and_metrics-common-configuration-parameters*
1.1 Common Configuration Parameters

The following parameters exist and have the same meaning in both the FSDP
and Megatron schemes.

     *dev_guide-model_dev-parameter_and_metrics-actor-optimizer-configuration*
1.1.1 Actor Optimizer Configuration

Parameter                             Default       Description
------------------------------------  ------------  --------------------------
actor_rollout_ref.actor.optim.lr      1.0e-06       Actor learning rate
actor_rollout_ref.actor.optim.lr_warmup_steps_ratio  0.0           Ratio of learning-rate
                                                    warmup steps to total
                                                    training steps
actor_rollout_ref.actor.optim.total_training_steps  -1            Total training steps; -1
                                                    means auto-computed
actor_rollout_ref.actor.optim.weight_decay  0.01          Weight decay, used to
                                                    prevent model overfitting
actor_rollout_ref.actor.optim.lr_warmup_steps  -1            Learning-rate warmup
                                                    steps; -1 means
                                                    auto-computed from ratio
actor_rollout_ref.actor.optim.betas   [0.9, 0.999]  First- and second-order
                                                    momentum coefficients of
                                                    the Adam optimizer
actor_rollout_ref.actor.optim.clip_grad  1.0           Gradient clipping
                                                    threshold
actor_rollout_ref.actor.optim.override_optimizer_config  null / {}     Override optimizer
                                                    configuration (null for
                                                    FSDP, {} for Megatron)

        *dev_guide-model_dev-parameter_and_metrics-actor-policy-configuration*
1.1.2 Actor Policy Configuration

Parameter                             Default                   Description
------------------------------------  ------------------------  ----------------------------
actor_rollout_ref.actor.strategy      fsdp / megatron           Training strategy; fsdp for
                                                                the FSDP scheme, megatron
                                                                for the Megatron scheme
actor_rollout_ref.actor.ppo_mini_batch_size  256                       Mini batch size for PPO
                                                                training
actor_rollout_ref.actor.ppo_micro_batch_size  null                      Micro batch size for PPO
                                                                training
actor_rollout_ref.actor.ppo_micro_batch_size_per_gpu  null                      PPO micro batch size per GPU
actor_rollout_ref.actor.use_dynamic_bsz  false                     Whether to use dynamic batch
                                                                size
actor_rollout_ref.actor.ppo_max_token_len_per_gpu  16384                     PPO max token length per GPU
actor_rollout_ref.actor.clip_ratio    0.2                       PPO clipping ratio, controls
                                                                the policy update magnitude;
                                                                typical range is [0.1, 0.3]
actor_rollout_ref.actor.clip_ratio_low  0.2                       PPO lower-bound clipping
                                                                ratio
actor_rollout_ref.actor.clip_ratio_high  0.2                       PPO upper-bound clipping
                                                                ratio
actor_rollout_ref.actor.tau_pos       1.0                       tau parameter for
                                                                positive-advantage clipping
actor_rollout_ref.actor.tau_neg       1.05                      tau parameter for
                                                                negative-advantage clipping
actor_rollout_ref.actor.freeze_vision_tower  false                     Whether to freeze the vision
                                                                tower (multimodal models)
actor_rollout_ref.actor.clip_ratio_c  3.0                       Upper-bound constant for the
                                                                clipping ratio
actor_rollout_ref.actor.loss_agg_mode  token-mean                Loss aggregation mode;
                                                                options include token-mean,
                                                                and so on
actor_rollout_ref.actor.loss_scale_factor  null                      Loss scale factor
actor_rollout_ref.actor.entropy_coeff  0                         Entropy regularization
                                                                coefficient, controls the
                                                                exploration level of the
                                                                policy
actor_rollout_ref.actor.calculate_entropy  false                     Whether to compute the
                                                                policy entropy
actor_rollout_ref.actor.use_kl_loss   false                     Whether to use KL divergence
                                                                loss
actor_rollout_ref.actor.use_prefix_grouper  false                     Whether to use the prefix
                                                                grouper
actor_rollout_ref.actor.use_torch_compile  true                      Whether to use torch.compile
                                                                for acceleration
actor_rollout_ref.actor.kl_loss_coef  0.001                     KL loss coefficient
actor_rollout_ref.actor.kl_loss_type  low_var_kl                KL loss type; options
                                                                include low_var_kl, and so
                                                                on
actor_rollout_ref.actor.ppo_epochs    1                         Number of PPO update epochs
actor_rollout_ref.actor.shuffle       false                     Whether to shuffle mini
                                                                batches during training
actor_rollout_ref.actor.data_loader_seed  42                        Data loader random seed
actor_rollout_ref.actor.grad_clip     1.0                       Gradient clipping value
actor_rollout_ref.actor.ulysses_sequence_parallel_size  1                         Ulysses sequence-parallel
                                                                size
actor_rollout_ref.actor.entropy_from_logits_with_chunking  false                     Whether to compute entropy
                                                                from logits in a chunked
                                                                manner
actor_rollout_ref.actor.entropy_from_logits_chunk_size  2048                      Chunk size for entropy
                                                                computation
actor_rollout_ref.actor.entropy_checkpointing  false                     Whether to use gradient
                                                                checkpointing for entropy
                                                                computation
actor_rollout_ref.actor.use_remove_padding  references                Whether to remove padding
                                      model.use_remove_padding
actor_rollout_ref.actor.calculate_sum_pi_squared  false                     Whether to compute the sum
                                                                of squared policy
                                                                probabilities
actor_rollout_ref.actor.sum_pi_squared_checkpointing  false                     Whether to use gradient
                                                                checkpointing for the
                                                                sum-of-squared-probabilities
                                                                computation
actor_rollout_ref.actor.use_fused_kernels  references                Whether to use fused kernels
                                      model.use_fused_kernels

         *dev_guide-model_dev-parameter_and_metrics-policy-loss-configuration*
1.1.3 Policy Loss Configuration

Parameter                             Default  Description
------------------------------------  -------  -------------------------------
actor_rollout_ref.actor.policy_loss.loss_mode  vanilla  Policy loss mode; options
                                               include vanilla, clip_cov,
                                               kl_cov, dppo_tv, dppo_kl, gspo,
                                               sapo, geo_mean, cispo, gpg,
                                               bypass_mode, reinforce_is, and
                                               so on
actor_rollout_ref.actor.policy_loss.clip_cov_ratio  0.0002   Covariance ratio for clip_cov
                                               mode
actor_rollout_ref.actor.policy_loss.clip_cov_lb  1.0      Covariance lower bound for
                                               clip_cov mode
actor_rollout_ref.actor.policy_loss.clip_cov_ub  5.0      Covariance upper bound for
                                               clip_cov mode
actor_rollout_ref.actor.policy_loss.kl_cov_ratio  0.0002   Covariance ratio for kl_cov
                                               mode
actor_rollout_ref.actor.policy_loss.ppo_kl_coef  0.1      PPO KL divergence coefficient

*dev_guide-model_dev-parameter_and_metrics-reference-fsdp-engine-configuration*
1.2.3 Reference FSDP Engine Configuration

Same structure as the Actor FSDP engine configuration. The main differences:

Parameter                             Default  Description
------------------------------------  -------  -------------------------------
actor_rollout_ref.ref.fsdp_config.forward_only  true     The Reference model only does
                                               forward computation

The remaining parameters (`wrap_policy`, `param_offload`,
`optimizer_offload`, `reshard_after_forward`, `fsdp_size`, `dtype`, and so
on) have the same default values as in the Actor FSDP engine configuration.

  *dev_guide-model_dev-parameter_and_metrics-critic-fsdp-engine-configuration*
1.2.4 Critic FSDP Engine Configuration

Same structure as the Actor FSDP engine configuration. The main differences:

Parameter                             Default  Description
------------------------------------  -------  -------------------------------
critic.model.fsdp_config.forward_only  false    The Critic model needs to be
                                               trained
critic.model.fsdp_config.use_remove_padding  false    The Critic does not remove
                                               padding

The remaining parameters have the same default values as in the Actor FSDP
engine configuration.

                                               forward computation

Megatron engine configuration (such as `param_offload`,
`tensor_model_parallel_size`, and so on).

*dev_guide-model_dev-parameter_and_metrics-critic-megatron-engine-configuration*

critic.megatron.forward_only   false    The Critic model needs to be trained

      *dev_guide-model_dev-parameter_and_metrics-training-metric-descriptions*
2. TRAINING METRIC DESCRIPTIONS

The log metrics printed at each iteration of the reinforcement-learning
algorithm are described below:

            *dev_guide-model_dev-parameter_and_metrics-basic-training-metrics*
2.1 Basic Training Metrics

Metric                           Description
-------------------------------  ---------------------------------------------
training/global_step             Current global training step
training/epoch                   Current training epoch

               *dev_guide-model_dev-parameter_and_metrics-actor-model-metrics*
2.2 Actor Model Metrics

Metric                         Description
-----------------------------  -----------------------------------------------
actor/pg_loss                  Policy gradient loss (PPO clip loss); the value
                               of the policy gradient objective function based
                               on the advantage function
actor/kl_loss                  KL divergence loss, measuring the deviation
                               between the current policy and the reference
                               policy (only printed when use_kl_loss=True)
actor/entropy                  Policy entropy, representing the randomness or
                               exploration capability of the policy (only
                               printed when calculate_entropy=True or
                               entropy_coeff!=0)
actor/grad_norm                Actor gradient norm (after clipping),
                               representing the overall magnitude of parameter
                               gradients during backpropagation
actor/lr                       Current Actor learning rate
actor/pg_clipfrac              The fraction where the PPO clipping mechanism
                               takes effect, reflecting the stability of the
                               policy update magnitude
actor/ppo_kl                   Actual KL divergence of the PPO algorithm
                               (current policy vs. old policy)
actor/pg_clipfrac_lower        PPO lower-bound clipping ratio (some loss_mode
                               have this metric)
actor/reward_kl_penalty        KL penalty value, the mean KL between the
                               current policy and the reference policy (only
                               printed when use_kl_in_reward=True)
actor/reward_kl_penalty_coeff  KL penalty coefficient beta (only printed when
                               use_kl_in_reward=True)
actor/kl_coef                  KL loss coefficient (only printed when
                               use_kl_loss=True)

                         takes effect, reflecting the stability of the
                         value-function update magnitude

critic/grad_norm         Critic gradient norm (after clipping)
critic/lr                Current Critic learning rate
critic/vf_explained_var  Value-function explained variance 1 -
                         Var(returns-values)/Var(returns) (only printed when
                         use_critic=True)

----------------------------------------------------------------------------
                              *verl-dev_guide-model_dev-transfer_to_npu_guide*
Guide to Migrating Models to NPU

Last updated: 05/14/2026

This guide provides developers with complete practical experience for
migrating models from GPU to NPU or independently adapting models on NPU,
covering the full workflow: preparation, component integration, precision
alignment, performance optimization, and long-run evaluation.

1. PREPARATION         *dev_guide-model_dev-transfer_to_npu_guide-preparation*

Set up a base runtime environment that supports NPU execution, ensuring that
the model loads normally and datasets can be read smoothly. This serves as
the foundation for subsequent migration debugging and end-to-end business
execution.

*dev_guide-model_dev-transfer_to_npu_guide-software-hardware-environment-and-dependency-configuration*
1.1 Software/Hardware Environment and Dependency Configuration

Refer to the official |verl-en-get_start-install_guidance|. If the versions
of the inference engines (vllm, vllm_ascend) and training engines (Megatron,
MindSpeed, transformers) that the model depends on differ from those in this
tutorial, use the versions actually adapted for the model.

1.2 Model Weights    *dev_guide-model_dev-transfer_to_npu_guide-model-weights*

BF16 is the default mixed-precision training data type for training backends
such as FSDP and Megatron in the VeRL framework. The Ascend NPU environment
uniformly uses BF16 as the baseline precision format, and weights need to be
aligned and dequantized to BF16. Currently, A2 and A3 models do not support
FP8 precision training and only support BF16 precision; the Ascend
950PR&950DT products will open up FP8 low-precision training capabilities in
later versions.

                  *dev_guide-model_dev-transfer_to_npu_guide-data-preparation*
1.3 Data Preparation

Following |verl-preparation-prepare_data|, preprocess the dataset into
parquet format: (1) ensure it contains the necessary fields for computing
reinforcement-learning rewards; (2) reading is faster.

             *dev_guide-model_dev-transfer_to_npu_guide-component-integration*
2. COMPONENT INTEGRATION

The VeRL framework adopts an architecture that decouples the inference
engine, training engine, and weight-synchronization bridge (Checkpoint
Engine), enabling deep separation of computation and data, which provides a
flexible extension foundation for migrating and adapting models to Ascend
NPU. When migrating and adapting models on NPU, it is recommended to first
complete the individual adaptation and verification of each component ---
the inference engine, training engine, and Megatron-Bridge --- and then
advance to integrating and debugging the full VeRL pipeline once each
component runs stably. For the specific feature support of different VeRL
inference and training backends on Ascend NPU, refer to the
|verl-en-feature_support-ascend_backend_features|.

       *dev_guide-model_dev-transfer_to_npu_guide-inference-engine-adaptation*
2.1 Inference Engine Adaptation

The VeRL inference engine adopts a layered architecture design. Through
abstract interfaces and the factory pattern, it flexibly supports multiple
mainstream inference backends such as vllm and sglang. During the migration
and adaptation from GPU to NPU, the inference engine adaptation is
recommended to follow this workflow:

Before running the full VeRL pipeline on NPU, it is recommended to refer to
the official model deployment tutorials of vllm-ascend
https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm-ascend/tree/main/docs/source/tutorials/models
and sglang
https://github.com/sgl-project/sglang/tree/main/docs/docs/basic_usage, and
first get the single-instance inference pipeline working. Fully verify basic
inference functionality such as model loading and initialization, normal
tokenizer loading, single-turn/batch generation, stop-token termination, and
long-context inference. After the underlying inference engine is stable and
usable, integrate it into the VeRL training workflow.

*dev_guide-model_dev-transfer_to_npu_guide-training-engine-selection-and-adaptation*
2.2 Training Engine Selection and Adaptation

The VeRL mainline code abstracts the training engine as the `Engine` class,
decoupling scheduling logic from the underlying training implementation
through a standardized interface layer. This architectural design allows
multiple training backends such as FSDP and Megatron to be flexibly
integrated in a plug-and-play manner without modifying VeRL's core
algorithms and scheduling logic, greatly reducing migration and adaptation
costs.

Currently, NPU has completed automatic device detection through the
`is_npu_available` interface and automatically applies the corresponding NPU
device adaptation patches. Now you only need to configure
model_engine=fsdp/megatron to switch the training backend to FSDP or
Megatron with one click. The system automatically loads the NPU adaptation
logic for the corresponding backend without additional code modification. In
VeRL, Ascend has adapted and optimized Megatron; for specific feature
configuration, refer to the verl-MindSpeed feature documentation
https://gitcode.com/Ascend/MindSpeed/blob/master/docs/en/user-guide/verl.md.

        *dev_guide-model_dev-transfer_to_npu_guide-megatron-bridge-adaptation*
2.3 Megatron-Bridge Adaptation

Megatron-Bridge is mainly used in the VeRL framework to perform
bidirectional conversion between the HuggingFace weights that the inference
engine depends on and the mcore weights required by Megatron-Core. This
feature can be enabled with the following configuration:

>python
    actor_rollout_ref.actor.megatron.use_mbridge=True
<
Megatron-Bridge has natively adapted a large number of mainstream model
structures in the community. For the supported list, refer to
supported model
https://github.com/NVIDIA-NeMo/Megatron-Bridge/blob/main/docs/models/README.md.
When migrating and adapting models in the Ascend NPU environment, basic
configuration can be completed based on existing community capabilities, but
some model-specific structures and scenarios still require supplementary
customized adaptation.

Taking the DSA (DeepSeek Sparse Attention) sparse attention structure as an
example, we introduce the customized adaptation method. Ascend MindSpeed
supports DSA capability based on the absorption matrix. This feature
requires splitting the original `linear_kv_up_proj` operator in Megatron
into two independent operators: `linear_k_up_proj` and `linear_v_up_proj`.
The weights required for the split need to be converted from the
HuggingFace-format `self_attn.kv_b_proj.weight`, but the aforementioned
native PR did not adapt this operator-splitting logic.

Therefore, the relevant weight-conversion logic must be manually adapted to
ensure the absorption matrix can be loaded and take effect normally. Only on
the basis that the absorption matrix is available can the
sparse_flash_attention
https://gitcode.com/cann/ops-transformer/tree/master/attention/sparse_flash_attention
and lightning_indexer
https://gitcode.com/cann/ops-transformer/tree/master/attention/lightning_indexer
fusion operators be properly enabled. By introducing these two fusion
operators, memory-access frequency can be greatly reduced, memory
utilization optimized, and computation performance improved, ultimately
improving the operational efficiency of large-model training and inference
pipelines while reducing resource overhead.

         *dev_guide-model_dev-transfer_to_npu_guide-full-pipeline-integration*
2.4 Full-Pipeline Integration

After completing the inference-engine adaptation verification and
training-engine adaptation development, refer to
|verl-dev_guide-model_dev-parameter_and_metrics| to configure the relevant
parameters of the inference engine and training engine according to actual
business requirements, and complete the full VeRL pipeline integration to
ensure the entire process runs stably.

               *dev_guide-model_dev-transfer_to_npu_guide-precision-alignment*
3. PRECISION ALIGNMENT

Precision-issue localization for large-model reinforcement learning involves
a complex pipeline with many influencing factors. Various precision issues
are typically introduced by problems in the training phase, inference phase,
and training-inference consistency. Precision alignment is the core key to
ensuring that the training pipeline is reproducible and issues are
debuggable.

For precision alignment in the training and inference phases, refer to the
official document: |verl-dev_guide-precision_analysis-precision_alignment|.
Therefore, this section does not reiterate the basic phase-alignment
workflow, but focuses on training-inference consistency scenarios, using the
msprobe precision tool to carry out precision-alignment practice and issue
localization and troubleshooting.

*dev_guide-model_dev-transfer_to_npu_guide-precision-monitoring-configuration*
3.1 Precision Monitoring Configuration

After the full pipeline runs through, enable the precision monitoring
parameter by setting `actor_rollout_ref.rollout.calculate_log_probs=True`.
During training, focus on the following key metrics to judge
training-inference consistency and model training stability:

- Training-inference consistency reference metrics:

  - `training/rollout_probs_diff_mean` (mean rollout probability
    difference). Under normal model convergence, this metric is recommended
    to stay within 0.01; if it consistently exceeds 0.01 or deviates
    significantly from the GPU baseline, a training-inference precision
    anomaly can be identified.
  - `training/rollout_probs_diff_max` (max rollout probability difference)
  - `training/rollout_actor_probs_pearson_corr` (Pearson correlation
    coefficient between rollout and actor probabilities)

- Model training stability metrics:

  - `actor/grad_norm`: monitor whether it shows an overall downward trend to
    judge whether the model is converging normally during training.

In addition, configure the parameter
`trainer.rollout_data_dir=./rollout_dump/` to save the intermediate Rollout
results during training. By manually inspecting the exported Rollout data,
you can verify whether model responses meet expectations and whether there
is garbled or repeated output, which further confirms on the surface that
the inference engine is adapted correctly.

         *dev_guide-model_dev-transfer_to_npu_guide-collecting-precision-data*
3.2 Collecting Precision Data

When training/rollout_probs_diff_mean exceeds the reasonable threshold of
0.01, or deviates significantly from the GPU baseline, further collect data
through the |verl-dev_guide-precision_analysis-precision_debugger| precision
tool for root-cause localization.

*dev_guide-model_dev-transfer_to_npu_guide-training-inference-discrepancy-investigation-and-alignment-practice*
3.3 Training-Inference Discrepancy Investigation and Alignment Practice

After data collection is complete, first read the `construction.json` file
for module-level data comparison. Ensure that the input data of
`layer.0.input_layernorm` is completely consistent, then verify module by
module and layer by layer to locate where the training and inference outputs
first diverge.

For large models, tiny numerical differences accumulate and amplify layer by
layer, causing the results of training and inference (rollout) to diverge
noticeably. It can even happen that the same token has output probabilities
of 0 and 1 in training and inference respectively. Therefore, every
discrepancy point must be aligned to be exactly equal as much as possible.

After locating the discrepancy node, determining the adaptation and
modification plan is also a key challenge. Because various open-source
communities have multiple different implementations of related modules, to
ensure the correctness of the model implementation logic, you should
reference authoritative source code and technical reports from multiple
sources and comprehensively determine the final alignment plan.

*dev_guide-model_dev-transfer_to_npu_guide-common-training-inference-inconsistencies*
3.3.1 Common Training-Inference Inconsistencies

In large-model reinforcement-learning practice, the typical root causes of
training-inference inconsistency can be categorized into the following five
classes:

1. Framework implementation inconsistencies: caused by different
   implementation logic in the training and inference frameworks. Sometimes
   these are “semantically correct” (for example, different
   operator-splitting approaches that are mathematically equivalent), and
   sometimes “semantically incorrect” (for example, a missing scaling factor
   or an extra operation). Strict verification against source code and
   technical reports is required.
2. Precision-type differences: for example, the training side uses BF16
   throughout, while the inference side implicitly upcasts to FP32 for some
   sensitive operators such as normalization and then downcasts, causing
   truncation errors.
3. Hyperparameter inconsistencies: for example, the hardcoded `eps` value in
   the LayerNorm module is not unified.
4. Parallelism strategies: tensor parallelism during training vs. continuous
   batching during inference causes differences in floating-point
   accumulation order.
5. Randomness control: implementation deviations of Dropout and sampling
   strategies between the training and inference phases.

Below are typical real cases of training-inference inconsistency encountered
during the GLM-5 model migration and adaptation.

*dev_guide-model_dev-transfer_to_npu_guide-case-1-framework-implementation-inconsistency-of-the-ffn-activation-function*
3.3.2 Case 1: Framework Implementation Inconsistency of the FFN Activation
Function

Comparing from top to bottom, the output discrepancy was located at the MLP
activation function of the first layer.

The inference side already uses the NPU-optimized `npu_swiglu` fusion
operator, but the training side still runs the native GLU small-operator
implementation.

- Root cause: Although the `swiglu` enable configuration has been added to
  the VeRL parameters, the Megatron-Bridge NPU adaptation PR did not
  explicitly configure `provider.bias_activation_fusion=True`, so the code
  did not enter the NPU fusion-operator branch.
- Fix: Add the configuration items in Megatron-Bridge so the training side
  correctly invokes the fusion operator:

  >text
      +actor_rollout_ref.actor.megatron.override_transformer_config.swiglu=True \
      +actor_rollout_ref.actor.megatron.override_transformer_config.use_fused_swiglu=True \
<

*dev_guide-model_dev-transfer_to_npu_guide-case-2-precision-and-hyperparameter-inconsistency-of-indexer-k-norm*
3.3.3 Case 2: Precision and Hyperparameter Inconsistency of indexer_k_norm

During strict alignment, a precision-type and hyperparameter inconsistency
was found at `indexer_k_norm`:

- Precision difference: the inference side upcasts to fp32 in LayerNorm with
  `F.layer_norm( x.float(), (self.dim,), self.weight, self.bias, self.eps).type_as(x)`,
  while the training-side Megatron implementation uses BF16. Tiny
  differences become non-negligible after accumulating across many layers.
- Fix: Unify the training-side code to add the upcast/downcast operations.
- Hyperparameter difference: on the GLM5 inference side, vllm inherits the
  DeepSeek-V3.2 logic, and the EPS value of `k_norm` is hardcoded to `1e-6`;
  whereas the training engine and the official technical report uniformly
  use `1e-5`.
- Fix: Modify the inference-side EPS to `1e-5` to align with the training
  side.

>python
    self.k_norm=LayerNorm(self.head_dim,eps=1e-6 -> 1e-5)
<

*dev_guide-model_dev-transfer_to_npu_guide-case-3-missing-and-redundant-logic-in-the-lightning-indexer-module*
3.3.4 Case 3: Missing and Redundant Logic in the lightning_indexer Module

Investigation found that the lightning_indexer module has inconsistent
implementations between the training and inference sides, specifically:

- Missing (inference-side omission): the inference side is missing the
  scaling logic for `weights`. Referring to the standard implementations in
  the Megatron training side, slime, and transformers, all include this
  scaling, so it was added on the inference side to align the forward pass:

>text
    weights, _ = self.weights_proj(x)
    +weights = weights * (self.n_head**-0.5) * (self.head_dim**-0.5)
<
- Redundant (extra erroneous implementation on the training side): the
  training-side Megatron implementation has an extra `rotate_activation`
  (Hadamard transform). After consulting extensive materials, this operation
  was confirmed to be dedicated to quantization scenarios and is an
  erroneous implementation in BF16 format. Following Transformer PR#45017
  https://github.com/huggingface/transformers/pull/45017, this redundant
  logic was removed on the training side.

>python
    class DSAIndexer(MegatronModule):
        def forward_with_scores(
    -        q = rotate_activation(q)
    -        k = rotate_activation(k)
<

*dev_guide-model_dev-transfer_to_npu_guide-general-routing-stability-solution-for-large-moe-models*
3.4 General Routing Stability Solution for Large MoE Models

In a typical RL training pipeline, efficient inference engines such as vLLM
are usually used to sample data, and the sampled data is then fed into
training frameworks such as Megatron for model training and optimization.

For conventional dense models, implementation and environment differences
between the inference and training frameworks only cause minor numerical
deviations; but for large MoE models, this issue is drastically amplified.
The core root cause lies in the MoE dynamic routing mechanism: tiny
framework-implementation and runtime-environment differences can cause the
same input token to be assigned to completely different expert combinations,
thereby following entirely different computation paths.

Such routing-decision inconsistencies may cause unstable RL training of MoE
models. It makes the “experience” obtained from the inference phase
completely different for the training phase, distorting the optimization
signal and ultimately leading to catastrophic consequences.

To solve this general problem, the industry has introduced the Routing
Replay mechanism. Its core idea is to lock the expert routing paths of a
specific phase, shielding routing decisions from the interference of minor
perturbations, thereby ensuring the stability of model training. Currently,
there are two mainstream variants, R2 and R3:

- (1) Vanilla Routing Replay (R2): (corresponds to
  `actor_rollout_ref.actor.megatron.router_replay.mode="R2"`, or
  `actor_rollout_ref.actor.veomni.router_replay.mode="R2"` for VeOmni)

  - Mechanism: In the gradient-update phase, it reproduces the expert paths
    computed by the training engine in the previous sampling phase.
  - Effect: mainly mitigates the impact of policy staleness on routing. As
    the policy is updated, the routing computed in the current forward pass
    may differ from the routing when the old data was generated; R2
    maintains the coherence of the optimization signal by replaying the old
    routing.

- (2) Rollout Routing Replay (R3): (corresponds to
  `actor_rollout_ref.actor.megatron.router_replay.mode="R3"`)

  - Mechanism: During sequence generation, it captures the routing
    distribution of the inference engine and replays it directly into the
    training engine.
  - Effect: simultaneously addresses both training-inference divergence and
    policy staleness. It ensures that the expert paths used to compute the
    loss in the training phase are absolutely consistent with the expert
    paths used when the inference results were actually generated.

Therefore, whether it is R2, which focuses on mitigating policy staleness,
or R3, which achieves full-pipeline alignment, the Routing Replay mechanism
effectively bridges the routing gap between the inference and training
frameworks. In the training-inference consistency alignment of large MoE
models, this mechanism has become a core means of ensuring precision
alignment and training stability. Currently, mainstream large models such as
DeepSeek-V3.2, GLM-5, and MiMo-V2 have all adopted the R3-mode Routing
Replay technique.

Therefore, for large MoE models, the R3 mode, which provides more thorough
alignment, is generally recommended in actual configuration:

>python
    actor_rollout_ref.actor.megatron.router_replay.mode="R3" \
    actor_rollout_ref.rollout.enable_rollout_routing_replay=True \
<
For the VeOmni backend, use
`actor_rollout_ref.actor.veomni.router_replay.mode="R3"` instead. The
top-level `actor.router_replay` has been removed and is no longer effective.

          *dev_guide-model_dev-transfer_to_npu_guide-performance-optimization*
4. PERFORMANCE OPTIMIZATION

When optimizing large-model RL (reinforcement learning) training performance
on Ascend NPU, basic configuration tuning can first refer to the official
document: perf_tuning.rst
https://github.com/verl-project/verl/blob/04833f01/docs/perf/perf_tuning.rst.
For more efficient optimization, it is recommended to follow the
standardized workflow of data collection -> bottleneck localization ->
configuration tuning -> iterative verification. This workflow can
significantly improve the throughput of core phases such as Rollout, Reward,
and Update, while effectively reducing resource idling and load imbalance.
For the specific operations of performance analysis and tuning, strictly
follow the official guides below:

1. |verl-dev_guide-performance-ascend_performance_analysis_guide|
2. |verl-dev_guide-performance-ascend_profiling|

*dev_guide-model_dev-transfer_to_npu_guide-inference-performance-optimization*
4.1 Inference Performance Optimization

The Rollout phase is the core inference stage of large-model RL training,
and its inference time occupies the vast majority of the entire training
pipeline. The following are common performance optimization methods for this
phase:

1. Enable graph mode: Graph mode compiles and optimizes the entire
   computation graph in advance, enabling deep optimizations such as
   operator fusion, memory reuse, and constant folding, which significantly
   improves execution efficiency.
2. CPU binding to accelerate operator dispatch: CPU binding can improve
   operator-dispatch efficiency. Since vllm-ascend v0.18.0rc1, this
   capability is enabled by default on ARM-architecture Ascend servers.
3. Configure the HCCL communication algorithm to AIV mode: Set the
   environment variable `HCCL_OP_EXPANSION_MODE` to `AIV` mode to specify
   that the orchestration and expansion logic of the communication algorithm
   runs on the Device-side Vector Core compute unit.
4. Enable async scheduling: This eliminates the gap between two consecutive
   execute_model executions by the Worker, allowing the Worker to directly
   fetch the already-scheduled SchedulerOutput for model inference without
   blocking and waiting for scheduling.

The corresponding configuration parameters are as follows:

>text
    # Enable graph mode
    actor_rollout_ref.rollout.enforce_eager=False
    +actor_rollout_ref.rollout.engine_kwargs.vllm.compilation_config.cudagraph_mode="FULL_DECODE_ONLY"
    +actor_rollout_ref.rollout.engine_kwargs.vllm.compilation_config.cudagraph_capture_sizes="[2, 4, 8, 16, 24, 32]"
    # CPU core binding
    ++actor_rollout_ref.rollout.engine_kwargs.vllm.additional_config.enable_cpu_binding=True
    # Enable async scheduling
    ++actor_rollout_ref.rollout.engine_kwargs.vllm.async_scheduling=True
<

 *dev_guide-model_dev-transfer_to_npu_guide-training-performance-optimization*
4.2 Training Performance Optimization

The Update phase of large-model RL training has characteristics such as
large sequence-length differences and high memory consumption. In addition
to basic operator fusion, it also requires combining sequence parallelism
and memory-computation trade-off strategies to break through bottlenecks.
Common training performance-optimization features can be enabled by
referring to the MindSpeed-verl documentation
https://gitcode.com/Ascend/MindSpeed/blob/master/docs/en/user-guide/verl.md.
The core optimization methods include:

1. Operator fusion: Enable fusion operators such as RoPE, SwiGLU, RMSNorm,
   and DSA. Operator fusion reduces computation overhead and memory usage,
   improving training efficiency.
2. Remove padding: In RL training, Response lengths vary widely, and the
   traditional Padding strategy leads to a large amount of invalid
   computation. Enabling Remove padding packs multiple short sequences to
   fill the Tensor, greatly improving the utilization (MFU) of the NPU
   compute units.

       *dev_guide-model_dev-transfer_to_npu_guide-evaluation-and-verification*
5. EVALUATION AND VERIFICATION

After training is complete, evaluation and verification must be performed on
the target dataset to ensure the model's business performance after
migration meets the standard. The evaluation steps are the same for
different models. The following uses GLM-5 as an example to detail the
evaluation workflow (using the AISBenchmark tool, which supports evaluation
with multiple inference backends such as vllm/sglang).

The evaluation uses the math dataset aime2025 and the graduate-level
professional science dataset gpqa, verifying that scores improve in the
target direction while no catastrophic knowledge forgetting occurs in
unrelated directions.

                  *dev_guide-model_dev-transfer_to_npu_guide-install-aisbench*
5.1 Install aisbench

>bash
    git clone https://gitee.com/aisbench/benchmark.git
    cd benchmark
    pip install -e .
<

   *dev_guide-model_dev-transfer_to_npu_guide-download-the-evaluation-dataset*
5.2 Download the Evaluation Dataset

>bash
    # Inside the Linux server, at the tool root path
    cd path/to/benchmark/ais_bench/datasets
    wget http://opencompass.oss-cn-shanghai.aliyuncs.com/datasets/data/aime2025.zip
    unzip aime2025.zip
    rm aime2025.zip
<

*dev_guide-model_dev-transfer_to_npu_guide-modify-the-aisbench-configuration-code-to-enable-vllm-sglang-inference-evaluation*
5.3 Modify the AISBench Configuration Code to Enable vllm/sglang Inference
Evaluation

Open the file
benchmark/ais_bench/benchmark/configs/models/vllm_api/vllm_api_stream_chat.py.
This is the inference evaluation configuration file. The output length
`max_out_len` is recommended to match the training `max_response_len`.

>text
    from ais_bench.benchmark.models import VLLMCustomAPIChat
    from ais_bench.benchmark.utils.postprocess.model_postprocessors import extract_non_reasoning_content

    models = [
        dict(
            attr="service",
            type=VLLMCustomAPIChat,
            abbr='vllm-api-general-chat',
            path="/path/to/GLM-5", # Change to the GLM-5 model path
            model="GLM-5",
            stream=True,
            request_rate = 0,
            use_timestamp=False,
            max_seq_len=2048,
            retry = 2,
            api_key="",
            host_ip = "localhost", # IP of the inference service
            host_port = 12890 , # Port of the inference service
            max_out_len = 8192,  # Maximum output tokens length
            batch_size=48, # Maximum inference concurrency
            trust_remote_code=False,
            generation_kwargs = dict(
                temperature = 0,
                seed = 1234,
            ),
            pred_postprocessor=dict(type=extract_non_reasoning_content)
        )
    ]
<

*dev_guide-model_dev-transfer_to_npu_guide-launch-the-multi-node-inference-server*
5.4 Launch the Multi-Node Inference Server

Refer to the vllm_ascend GLM5 Guide
https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm-ascend/blob/main/docs/source/tutorials/models/GLM5.md
to launch the two-node A3 inference service. `host_port` should be
consistent with the configuration in the previous section, and
`max_model_len` should be set to the sum of `max_prompt_length` and
`max_response` during training.

   *dev_guide-model_dev-transfer_to_npu_guide-launch-the-vllm-evaluation-task*
5.5 Launch the vLLM Evaluation Task

Run the following command to launch the online inference evaluation task,
which invokes the deployed vLLM inference backend and loads the
corresponding model configuration to complete the automated evaluation:

>text
    ais_bench --models vllm_api_stream_chat --datasets aime2025_gen_0_shot_chat_prompt
<
After training, the model's core capability metrics achieved stable
improvement: the evaluation score on the AIME2025 math-reasoning dataset
rose steadily, and continuous score gains were also achieved on the GPQA
graduate-level professional science dataset, with no knowledge degradation
or catastrophic forgetting. The training optimization effect met
expectations.

Evaluation Dataset                   GLM5-base  10step  15step  40step  50step
-----------------------------------  ---------  ------  ------  ------  ------
aime2025                             47.5       49.17   49.17   48.33   52.5
gpqa                                 64.65      68.81   68.43   69.07   71.21

6. SUMMARY                 *dev_guide-model_dev-transfer_to_npu_guide-summary*

This guide comprehensively covers the full workflow practice of migrating
large models from GPU to Ascend NPU or independently adapting them on NPU.
It is divided into five key phases: environment setup, component
integration, precision alignment, performance optimization, and evaluation
and verification, providing developers with actionable, reusable operation
guides and issue-resolution solutions.

In the preparation phase, focus on controlling environment-dependency
versions, model-weight precision, and dataset format to lay the foundation
for subsequent adaptation. In the component-integration phase, follow the
principle of verifying single components first before full-pipeline
integration, prioritizing the stable adaptation of the inference engine,
training engine, and weight-conversion tool, and completing customized
modifications for special model structures. Precision alignment is the core
of migration and adaptation; focus on monitoring training-inference
consistency metrics, and resolve common discrepancies such as framework
implementation and precision types through module-by-module investigation.
MoE models need the Routing Replay mechanism enabled to ensure training
stability. Performance optimization should follow the standardized workflow,
focusing on the core inference and training phases, and improving efficiency
and reducing resource consumption through graph mode and operator fusion.
Finally, standardized evaluation and verification ensures that the model's
business performance meets the standard after migration, with no knowledge
degradation.

Overall, following the workflow in this guide can effectively reduce the
cost of NPU migration and adaptation, avoid common pitfalls, and achieve
stable, efficient operation of large models on Ascend NPU.

                *verl-dev_guide-performance-ascend_performance_analysis_guide*
Ascend Performance Analysis Guide

Last updated: 02/24/2026.

          *dev_guide-performance-ascend_performance_analysis_guide-background*
BACKGROUND

Following the release of DeepSeek-R1, large-model reinforcement learning
(RL) training has attracted widespread attention. In the Ascend NPU
environment, the verl framework has accumulated rich experience in
performance tuning. This document systematically summarizes the methodology
for performance data collection and analysis, aiming to help developers use
the MindStudio toolchain more efficiently and achieve performance
optimization in reinforcement learning scenarios.

*dev_guide-performance-ascend_performance_analysis_guide-overview-of-the-reinforcement-learning-computation-flow*
Overview of the Reinforcement Learning Computation Flow

1. Rollout: The policy (actor) model performs inference based on the input
   prompt sequence to generate a response (response sequence).
2. ref logprob: Based on the prompt and the generated response, the
   reference model computes the ref logprob used for KL divergence
   computation.
3. logprob: Based on the prompt and the generated response, the actor model
   computes the logprob used for importance sampling.
4. reward: Based on the prompt and the generated response, the reward model
   evaluates the reward value R_N.
5. update: Based on the computed R_N, ref logprob, and logprob, compute the
   optimization function and policy gradient, and update the actor model.
