*dev_guide-performance-ascend_performance_analysis_guide-performance-analysis-cases*
PERFORMANCE ANALYSIS CASES

For detailed performance analysis, profiling must enable level1; otherwise,
key operator information will be missing.

*dev_guide-performance-ascend_performance_analysis_guide-host-bound-diagnosis*
1. Host Bound Diagnosis

Host bound refers to the phenomenon where the total CPU task volume exceeds
that of the NPU, resulting in idles (bubbles) during NPU execution. You can
determine this by looking at the Host2Device synchronization lines: if the
lines are all skewed, it proves that the set signal comes earlier than the
wait signal, and the NPU executes as soon as it is ready, which is actually
device bound:

If diagnosed as host bound, we can open the CPU side and find the dispatch
time of each operator. Note that you need to find the cumulative value of
all CPU time, not a single layer, because the first call takes a long time.
For example, for GmmSwigluQuant in the figure below, the first call on the
CPU takes 1ms, and each subsequent call only takes 200μs.

At this point, some operators are carrying a heavy load while others are
dragging behind, and the latter outnumber the former. We prioritize finding
the top operators whose host time exceeds the device time; these are the
operators dragging things behind, and they can be handed over to the
operator team for focused analysis.

*dev_guide-performance-ascend_performance_analysis_guide-network-architecture-rationality-analysis*
2. Network Architecture Rationality Analysis

Sometimes the model network is not organized in the most efficient way, and
this is very easy to identify in profiling. The analysis approach is
introduced below with examples.

Generally speaking, the large hot operators in an LLM are the matrix
multiplication computations in Attention and FFN. Together, they can account
for 70%+ of the computation time in prefill and 50%+ in decode. If the
overall time proportion does not meet expectations, or new faces appear in
the profiling, or there are too many concatenation-type operators, it is
worth analyzing the model network to see whether the operators are being
used incorrectly. Concatenation-type operators in particular are worth
analyzing one by one.

For concatenation-type operators like slice/split/concat and conversion
operators like transpose/cast, their presence is often caused by the
preceding and following operators not being directly compatible. If the
preceding operator can directly perform tail-processing on the output, it
can often save the startup overhead of one operator and one redundant
read/write. However, such a change may not conform to the basic design
principles of operators.

As a positive example, suppose the output shape of a Matmul is [m, n0 + n1],
and we then attach two slices, both taking this [m, n0 + n1] tensor as input
and producing outputs of [m, n0] and [m, n1] respectively. The first
optimization idea is to change the two slices into a single split, which
roughly halves the time and allows the [m, n0 + n1] memory to be released
earlier. A further optimization idea is to split the matrix multiplication
weight from [k, n0 + n1] into [k, n0] and [k, n1], splitting the original
matrix multiplication task into two (provided the combined time of the two
is not much worse than before, and the core-splitting strategy does not
cause issues), thereby completely eliminating the slice/split operation.

As a negative example, consider Rmsnorm(fp16)+Cast(fp16->fp32)+Matmul(fp32).
Although Rmsnorm has fp16 inputs and outputs, internally it computes in fp32
to preserve the precision of accumulation. If the Cast is fused into
Rmsnorm, the Rmsnorm---which already uses fp32 internally---can save the
trailing fp32->fp16 cast, and combined with the eliminated Cast, we save two
casts and avoid one precision loss. Although this seems to improve both
precision and performance, an Rmsnorm with fp16 input and fp32 output
violates the principle (the core input and output should be of the same data
type). Unless we can frequently find such a structure in widely-used
open-source models, proving its universality, the operator team will not
allow such an operator.

*dev_guide-performance-ascend_performance_analysis_guide-operator-performance-initial-diagnosis*
3. Operator Performance Initial Diagnosis

You need to use `"./ASCEND_PROFILER_OUTPUT/operator_details.csv"` for
analysis to determine whether an operator has a performance issue.

The Profiling tool computes the average busy time of these pipelines on
different cores (xxx_time), divides it by the full kernel time of the
slowest core (task_duration), and obtains the pipeline utilization
(xxx_ratio). Although these pipelines depend on each other and the
transfer-type pipelines compete for bandwidth, as long as the operator is
properly designed, they can be mutually masked. Therefore, we can
preliminarily consider that when an operator's execution time is large
enough, it should form a bound on one pipeline, that is, the utilization
should be high enough. Empirically, when a single operator takes 50μs, it
can be considered that the operator should be on the bound pipeline,
achieving 80%+ occupancy.

Taking the figure below as an example, the first row is an FA operator and
the second row is a Matmul operator. FA achieves 88.1% utilization on the
vec pipeline, and the Matmul operator achieves 89.8% utilization on the mac
pipeline. Their performance can be considered acceptable.

*dev_guide-performance-ascend_performance_analysis_guide-affinity-shape-adjustment*
4. Affinity Shape Adjustment

For a model, we cannot control the hyperparameters, but we can control
factors such as concurrency, weight format, and splitting strategy to
accommodate the operator and let it perform at its best. This section
focuses on two aspects---operator transfer efficiency and load
balancing---and discusses directions worth trying on the model side.

*dev_guide-performance-ascend_performance_analysis_guide-transfer-efficiency-affinity-shape*
4.1 Transfer-Efficiency-Affinity Shape

mte2 is a pipeline whose own efficiency is severely affected by shape. To
ensure maximum transfer efficiency for mte2, at least one of the following
two conditions must be met:

(1) The transferred matrix uses nz as format (optimal) (2) The tail axis of
the transferred matrix is 512B-aligned and not an integer multiple of 16KB
(approximately optimal)

For weight matrices, in the inference phase---especially decode---we usually
satisfy (1), and in the training phase we usually satisfy (2). If we cannot
achieve (1), we should accommodate (2). Typical approaches include:

1. If (1) is not achieved and the matrix's leading axis is affinity-friendly
   while the tail axis is not, transpose it.
2. Adjust the TP splitting strategy to avoid an unfriendly tail axis.

*dev_guide-performance-ascend_performance_analysis_guide-load-balancing-affinity-shape*
4.2 Load-Balancing-Affinity Shape

When the operator shape is small, due to operator semantics, we may not be
able to utilize all cores, or even with all cores enabled, load balancing is
poor. This subsection mainly analyzes the small shapes in the decode phase.

First, we clarify how many cores the current NPU card has. If you are not
sure, the numbers like 20 and 40 in the profiling output indicate 20 cores;
otherwise, it is 24 cores. Here, 24 cores actually represents a group
consisting of one cube and two vectors. We can consider it as one cube
acting as the master core, with two vectors as slave cores. If an operator
is a pure vector operator, the concept of groups no longer applies; the 40
or 48 vector cores act as master cores and directly take logical tasks
independently.

For vector operators in LLMs, one common core-splitting strategy is to split
along the highest dimension, that is, the batch dimension, which is common
in norm-type and dynamic-quantization-type operators that perform reduction
on low dimensions (also called tail axis). The other is to flatten
everything, allowing operators to be split very finely, such as elementwise
operators. For the first type, we can focus on load balancing issues on the
model side. For example, if we set a batch of 48 but the hardware has 40
vector cores, these 40 cores will loop twice, and in the second loop most
cores will have nothing to do. This batch size can be considered unfriendly.
If the batch is set to 64 or 80, performance is expected to be lossless. In
the same situation, if it is a 48-core card, we can consider this a very
friendly batch size.

For cube-type operators, the common core-splitting strategy is to split M
and N by base (the K axis is the accumulation axis, and splitting on it
introduces determinism issues). The most common blocking is baseM=128,
baseN=256. In the decode phase, the time can basically be considered as all
spent transferring weights, because the activation M is extremely small, and
the M direction most likely has only one block, so the right matrix only
needs to be transferred once. Therefore, within the range of M≤128, we can
freely increase M with basically no performance loss. If M exceeds 128,
(128, 256] can be considered the next performance tier. Besides M, the tasks
split on the N axis also affect operator affinity. Taking the MLA
preprocessing in deepseekR1 as an example, it uses the same activation
(shape [batch_size, 7168]) to do matrix multiplication with two weights
(shapes [7168, 1536] and [7168, 576]). When batch_size cannot be made large,
even if baseN is reduced to 128, the N axis still cannot use up all cores.
So the time of these two matrix multiplications each is approximately equal
to the time of multiplying the combined N axis of the weights (shape [7168,
2112]). If we only consider model competitiveness, we prefer to merge these
two weights; otherwise, the bandwidth utilization of both small matrix
multiplications will be very poor.

For Attention operators, the common core-splitting strategy is q_seqlen,
batch_size, and kv_headnum. In the incremental phase, q_seqlen is merged by
MTP and GQA multiples, but usually still does not exceed 128, so a second
task cannot be split out, and the parallelism is basically batch_size *
kv_headnum.

Overall, based on shape information and operator category, we can identify
whether an operator has a load balancing issue, and therefore anticipate our
splitting-strategy choices and the batch strategy for maximum throughput.

----------------------------------------------------------------------------
Profiling Collection Guide       *verl-dev_guide-performance-ascend_profiling*

Last updated: 07/13/2026.

This is a tutorial for data collection using the GRPO or DAPO algorithm
based on the FSDP or MindSpeed (Megatron) backend on Ascend devices.

CONFIGURATION           *dev_guide-performance-ascend_profiling-configuration*

Use two levels of profile settings to control data collection:

- Global collection control: Use parameters in
  verl/trainer/config/ppo_trainer.yaml (FSDP) or
  verl/trainer/config/ppo_megatron_trainer.yaml (MindSpeed) to control the
  collection mode and steps.
- Role profile control: Use parameters in each role to control collection
  and other parameters.

            *dev_guide-performance-ascend_profiling-global-collection-control*
Global Collection Control

Use parameters in ppo_trainer.yaml to control the collection steps and mode:

- global_profiler: Control the ranks and mode of profiling

  - tool: The profiling tool to use. Options are nsys, npu, torch,
    torch_memory.

    - nsys: NVIDIA's official system-level performance analysis tool.
    - npu: Huawei Ascend chip's native performance analysis tool.
    - torch: PyTorch framework's built-in profiler.
    - torch_memory: PyTorch's memory trace analyzer (based on the memory
      history snapshot functionality).

  - steps: This parameter can be set to a list containing the collection
    steps, for example [2, 4], which means step 2 and step 4 will be
    collected. If set to null, no collection occurs.
  - save_path: The path to save the collected data. Default is
    “outputs/profile”.

                *dev_guide-performance-ascend_profiling-role-profiler-control*
Role Profiler Control

In each role's `profiler` field, you can control the collection mode for
that role.

- enable: Whether to enable profiling for this role.
- all_ranks: Whether to collect data from all ranks.
- ranks: A list of ranks to collect data from. If empty, no data is
  collected.
- tool_config: Configuration for the profiling tool used by this role.

Use parameters in each role's `profiler.tool_config.npu` to control specific
collection behavior:

- level: Collection level -- options are level_none, level0, level1, and
  level2

  - level_none: Disables all level-based data collection (turns off
    profiler_level).
  - level0: Collects high-level application data, underlying NPU data, and
    operator execution details on the NPU. After balancing data volume and
    analytical capability, level0 is the recommended default configuration.
  - level1: Extends level0 by adding CANN-layer AscendCL data and AI Core
    performance metrics on the NPU.
  - level2: Extends level1 by adding CANN-layer Runtime data and AI CPU
    metrics.

- contents: A list of options to control the collection content, for example
  npu, cpu, memory, shapes, module, stack.

  - npu: Whether to collect device-side performance data.
  - cpu: Whether to collect host-side performance data.
  - memory: Whether to enable memory analysis.
  - shapes: Whether to record tensor shapes.
  - module: Whether to record framework-layer Python call stack information.
    Compared to stack, module is more recommended for recording call stack
    information because it incurs lower performance overhead.
  - stack: Whether to record operator call stack information.

- analysis: Whether to enable automatic data parsing.
- discrete: Whether to use discrete mode.
- schedule: Configures collection windows. verl advances the schedule by
  calling `profiler.step()` once per Actor/Critic update mini-batch. Takes
  effect only when `active > 0`:

  - skip_first: Number of initial mini-batches to ignore before the first
    cycle begins.
  - wait: Mini-batches to idle (no collection) at the start of each cycle.
  - warmup: Mini-batches to trace but discard, letting the profiler
    stabilize, each cycle.
  - active: Mini-batches to actively record each cycle. Set `0` (default) to
    disable scheduling. Negative values are not supported.
  - repeat: Number of cycles to record. `0` (default) repeats until
    profiling stops.

  With `discrete=False`, the scheduled profiler starts before log-prob
  computation and controls collection for all stages it covers, not just
  update mini-batches. For example, with `wait=1`, the initial log-prob
  computation runs in the wait phase and its trace is absent. Initial
  `skip_first` or `warmup` phases can likewise prevent earlier stages from
  being retained. With `discrete=True`, only Actor update is scheduled;
  log-prob stages are collected in full.

  For the Critic, scheduling is supported only with `discrete=False`. With
  `discrete=True`, each Critic mini-batch is collected in full and the
  schedule is ignored, even when `active > 0`.

- profile_token_start: Effective only for the rollout role; defines the
  start response-token index for rollout decoding collection. It is applied
  only when valid (0-based, `profile_token_end > profile_token_start`, and
  the window is within the response length).
- profile_token_end: Effective only for the rollout role; defines the stop
  response-token index (exclusive) for rollout decoding collection. It is
  applied only when valid (0-based,
  `profile_token_end > profile_token_start`, and the window is within the
  response length).

EXAMPLES                     *dev_guide-performance-ascend_profiling-examples*

                 *dev_guide-performance-ascend_profiling-disabling-collection*
Disabling Collection

>yaml
    global_profiler:
      steps: null # disable profile
<

                *dev_guide-performance-ascend_profiling-end-to-end-collection*
End-to-End Collection

>yaml
    global_profiler:
       steps: [1, 2, 5]
       save_path: ./outputs/profile
    actor_rollout_ref:
       actor:  # Set the profiler collection configuration parameters for the actor role
          profiler:
             enable: True
             all_ranks: True
             tool_config:
                npu:
                   discrete: True
                   contents: [npu, cpu]  # Control collection list, default cpu, npu; can configure memory, shapes, module, and so on
<

*dev_guide-performance-ascend_profiling-separation-of-training-and-inference-phases*
Separation of Training and Inference Phases

>yaml
    global_profiler:
       steps: [1, 2, 5]
       save_path: ./outputs/profile
    actor_rollout_ref:
       actor:
          profiler:
             enable: True  # Set to True to collect the training phase
             all_ranks: False
             ranks: [0]  # Global Rank 0
             tool_config:
                npu:
                   discrete: True
                   contents: [npu, cpu]
                   # Optional: sample mini-batches via schedule; without it, all actor mini-batches are collected
                   schedule:
                      skip_first: 0
                      wait: 0
                      warmup: 1  # warm up one mini-batch
                      active: 1  # record the second mini-batch
                      repeat: 1  # stop after one cycle
       rollout:
          profiler:
             enable: True  # Set to True to collect the inference phase
             all_ranks: False
             ranks: [0]  # Global GPU rank(s); each is mapped to the replica that owns it
             tool_config:
                npu:
                   discrete: True  # Discrete mode must be enabled in Agent Loop mode
                   # Optional: lightweight collection of inference data, collecting by response token interval; when start/stop are not set, the entire rollout phase is collected
                   profile_token_start: 30
                   profile_token_end: 60
       # ref follow actor settings
<

QUICK START               *dev_guide-performance-ascend_profiling-quick-start*

Disabling Collection              *dev_guide-performance-ascend_profiling-id1*

>python
    global_profiler.steps=null
<

End-to-End Collection             *dev_guide-performance-ascend_profiling-id2*

>python
    global_profiler.tool=npu
    global_profiler.steps="[1, 2, 5]" # Collection steps
    global_profiler.save_path=./outputs/profile
    actor_rollout_ref.actor.profiler.enable=True
    actor_rollout_ref.actor.profiler.all_ranks=False
    actor_rollout_ref.actor.profiler.ranks="[0]" # Only collect rank 0
    actor_rollout_ref.actor.profiler.tool_config.npu.discrete=True # Discrete mode is recommended; data of each phase is stored separately
    actor_rollout_ref.actor.profiler.tool_config.npu.contents="['npu','cpu']" # Control collection list, default cpu, npu; can configure memory, shapes, module, and so on
    actor_rollout_ref.actor.profiler.tool_config.npu.level=level1
    actor_rollout_ref.actor.profiler.tool_config.npu.analysis=False # Disable automatic data parsing
    # rollout & ref follow actor settings
<

*dev_guide-performance-ascend_profiling-lightweight-collection-of-inference-data*
Lightweight Collection of Inference Data

>python
    global_profiler.tool=npu
    global_profiler.steps="[1, 2, 5]" # Collection steps
    global_profiler.save_path=./outputs/profile
    actor_rollout_ref.actor.profiler.enable=True
    actor_rollout_ref.actor.profiler.all_ranks=False
    actor_rollout_ref.actor.profiler.ranks="[0]" # Only collect rank 0
    actor_rollout_ref.actor.profiler.tool_config.npu.discrete=True # Discrete mode is recommended; data of each phase is stored separately
    actor_rollout_ref.actor.profiler.tool_config.npu.contents="['npu','cpu']" # Control collection list, default cpu, npu; can configure memory, shapes, module, and so on
    actor_rollout_ref.actor.profiler.tool_config.npu.level=level1
    actor_rollout_ref.actor.profiler.tool_config.npu.analysis=False # Disable automatic data parsing

    # Optional: sample mini-batches via schedule; without it, all actor mini-batches are collected
    actor_rollout_ref.actor.profiler.tool_config.npu.schedule.skip_first=0
    actor_rollout_ref.actor.profiler.tool_config.npu.schedule.wait=0
    actor_rollout_ref.actor.profiler.tool_config.npu.schedule.warmup=1  # warm up one mini-batch
    actor_rollout_ref.actor.profiler.tool_config.npu.schedule.active=1  # record the second mini-batch
    actor_rollout_ref.actor.profiler.tool_config.npu.schedule.repeat=1  # stop after one cycle

    actor_rollout_ref.rollout.profiler.enable=True
    actor_rollout_ref.rollout.profiler.all_ranks=False
    actor_rollout_ref.rollout.profiler.ranks="[0]" # Only collect rank 0
    # Optional: lightweight collection of inference data, collecting by response token interval; when start/stop are not set, the entire rollout phase is collected
    actor_rollout_ref.rollout.profiler.tool_config.npu.profile_token_start=30
    actor_rollout_ref.rollout.profiler.tool_config.npu.profile_token_end=60
    # ref follow actor settings
<
Agent Loop Mode Notes:

In |verl-advance-agent_loop| mode, performance data for the Rollout phase
must be collected using discrete mode. In this case, the Profiler is
triggered by the inference engine backend.

1. Rank Definition: ranks in the Rollout configuration are global GPU ranks
   (the same as the training roles). Because each rollout replica spans
   `world_size = tensor_model_parallel_size * data_parallel_size * pipeline_model_parallel_size`
   GPUs, each listed rank is mapped to the replica that owns it
   (`replica = rank // world_size`) and the whole replica is profiled; for
   example, with `tp=8`, `ranks: [0, 8]` profiles the replicas holding
   global ranks 0 and 8 (that is replica 0 and replica 1).
2. Inference Engine Support: Currently, vLLM and SGLang engines are
   supported without additional settings. Specific details are as follows:

   - vLLM Engine: Automatically collects AsyncLLM scheduling stacks and
     inference process performance data. Does not support setting analysis
     (defaults to no parsing, requires offline parsing) and profiler_level
     (defaults to level1).
   - SGLang Engine: Automatically collects inference process performance
     data. Does not support the memory option in contents. Does not support
     setting analysis (defaults to parsing enabled) and profiler_level
     (defaults to level0).

Fully Async Policy Mode Notes:

1. In Fully Async Policy
   https://verl.readthedocs.io/en/latest/advance/fully_async.html mode,
   `global_profiler.steps` refers to the step after each `update_weights`
   round, which is consistent with synchronous mode, rather than a
   per-mini-batch step within a single training round.
2. Because it reuses the AgentLoop collection capabilities, the notes for
   Fully Async Policy
   https://verl.readthedocs.io/en/latest/advance/fully_async.html mode are
   the same as for AgentLoop.

VISUALIZATION           *dev_guide-performance-ascend_profiling-visualization*

Collected data is stored in the user-defined save_path and can be visualized
using the MindStudio Insight
https://www.hiascend.com/document/detail/en/mindstudio/latest/GUI_baseddevelopmenttool/MindStudioInsight/docs/en/install_guide/mindstudio_insight_install_guide.md
tool.

Additionally, in a Linux environment, the MindStudio Insight tool is
provided as a JupyterLab plugin
https://www.hiascend.com/document/detail/en/mindstudio/latest/GUI_baseddevelopmenttool/MindStudioInsight/docs/en/install_guide/mindstudio_insight_install_guide.md,
offering a more intuitive and highly interactive operation interface. The
advantages of the JupyterLab plugin are as follows:

- Seamless integration: Supports running the MindStudio Insight tool
  directly within the Jupyter environment, with no need to switch platforms
  or copy data from the server, enabling data to be collected and used
  immediately.
- Fast startup: MindStudio Insight can be launched quickly through the
  JupyterLab command line or graphical interface.
- Smooth operation: In a Linux environment, launching MindStudio Insight
  through JupyterLab effectively resolves the lag issues seen with
  full-package communication, significantly improving the operation
  experience.
- Remote access: Supports remotely launching MindStudio Insight. You can
  connect to the service through a local browser for direct visual analysis,
  easing the difficulty of uploading and downloading data during large-model
  training or inference.

If the analysis parameter is set to False, offline parsing is required after
collection:

>python
    import torch_npu
    # Set profiler_path to the parent directory of the "localhost.localdomain_<PID>_<timestamp>_ascend_pt" directory
    torch_npu.profiler.profiler.analyse(profiler_path=profiler_path)
<

*dev_guide-performance-ascend_profiling-advanced-guide-fine-grained-collection*
ADVANCED GUIDE: FINE-GRAINED COLLECTION

            *dev_guide-performance-ascend_profiling-background-and-challenges*
Background and Challenges

Although the configuration-based collection method described above is
convenient, it faces challenges in training scenarios with Long Context or
Large Global Batch Size. Within a complete training step (Step), model
computation exhibits high-frequency and repetitive characteristics:

1. Rollout phase: Sequence generation (Generate Sequence) is an
   autoregressive process involving thousands of forward computations of the
   Decoder model.
2. Training phase: To control peak memory usage, verl typically adopts a
   Micro-Batch strategy, splitting the large data stream into multiple
   micro-batches for computation.

   - compute_log_prob (Actor/Ref): Involves multiple rounds of pure forward
     propagation.
   - update_policy (Actor/Critic): Involves multiple rounds of forward and
     backward propagation.

These characteristics cause full profiling to produce massive and repetitive
operator records. As shown in the figure below:

Even with `discrete` mode enabled, a single stage's performance data file
can still reach several TB, leading to parsing failures or visualization
tool lag.

      *dev_guide-performance-ascend_profiling-solution-critical-path-sampling*
Solution: Critical Path Sampling

To address the above problems, we can adopt a Critical Path Sampling
strategy: Based on the API provided by torch_npu.profiler
https://www.hiascend.com/document/detail/en/Pytorch/latest/apiref/customapi/docs/en/custom_APIs/torch_npu-profiler/torch_npu-profiler-profile.md,
directly modify the Python source code to collect only representative data
segments (such as specific Decode Steps or the first Micro-Batch).

  Important Notes

  1. This chapter involves direct source code modification. It is
     recommended to back up files before modification and restore them after
     debugging.
  2. When using code instrumentation for collection, be sure to disable
     global collection (`global_profiler: steps: null`) in
     `ppo_trainer.yaml` or `ppo_megatron_trainer.yaml` to avoid Profiler
     conflicts.

*dev_guide-performance-ascend_profiling-add-a-script-to-control-collection-granularity*
1. Add a Script to Control Collection Granularity

>bash
    export PROFILE_STEP=2 # Collect specified steps
    export ROLLOUT_PROFILE=true
    export UPDATE_PROFILE=true
    export WITH_MODULES=false # Collect Python call stack
    export WITH_STACK=false # Collect operator call stack
    export WITH_MEMORY=false # Collect memory
    export WITH_SHAPE=true # Collect tensor shapes
    export PROFILE_RANKS=0 # Collect rank 0
    export UPDATE_PROFILE_PATH="./outputs/update_profile"
    export ROLLOUT_PROFILE_PATH="./outputs/rollout_profile"
<

*dev_guide-performance-ascend_profiling-fine-grained-collection-in-the-rollout-phase*
2. Fine-grained Collection in the Rollout Phase

For vLLM or SGLang inference engines, we can control the `schedule`
parameter to collect model forward-propagation performance data for specific
tokens.

vLLM Engine

- Reference Version: vLLM v0.18.0, vLLM-Ascend v0.18.1
- Modified File: `vllm-ascend/vllm_ascend/worker/worker.py`

>diff
        class NPUWorker(WorkerBase):

            def __init__(self, *args, **kwargs):
                # ... existing code ...

    +           # Profile collection
    +           import os
    +           import torch_npu
    +           if os.environ.get('ROLLOUT_PROFILE', "false") == "true":
    +               # Initialize profiler
    +               import torch_npu
    +               experimental_config = torch_npu.profiler._ExperimentalConfig(
    +                   profiler_level=torch_npu.profiler.ProfilerLevel.Level1,
    +               )
    +               self.profiler_npu = torch_npu.profiler.profile(
    +                   activities=[torch_npu.profiler.ProfilerActivity.CPU, torch_npu.profiler.ProfilerActivity.NPU],
    +                   with_modules=os.environ.get('WITH_MODULES', "false") == "true",
    +                   profile_memory=os.environ.get('WITH_MEMORY', "false") == "true",
    +                   record_shapes=os.environ.get('WITH_SHAPE', "false") == "true",
    +                   with_stack=os.environ.get('WITH_STACK', "false") == "true",
    +                   experimental_config=experimental_config,
    +                   # Skip the first 29 steps, warmup 1 step, collect 30 steps, repeat 1 time.
    +                   schedule=torch_npu.profiler.schedule(wait=29, warmup=1, active=30, repeat=1),
    +                   on_trace_ready=torch_npu.profiler.tensorboard_trace_handler(os.environ.get('ROLLOUT_PROFILE_PATH'), analyse_flag=True)  # Data save path, whether to parse online
    +               )
    +               self.profiler_npu.start()

                # ... existing code ...

            def execute_model(self, scheduler_output=None, intermediate_tensors=None, **kwargs):
                # ... existing code ...
                output = self.model_runner.execute_model(scheduler_output,
                                                    intermediate_tensors)

    +           import os
    +           if os.environ.get('ROLLOUT_PROFILE', "false") == "true":
    +               self.profiler_npu.step()  # Drive schedule to collect partial decode steps

SGLang Engine

- Reference Version: SGLang master branch
- Modified File: `sglang/python/sglang/srt/model_executor/model_runner.py`

>diff
        # ... existing imports ...
    +   import torch_npu

        class ModelRunner:

            def __init__(self, *args, **kwargs):
                # ... existing init code ...

    +           # Profile collection
    +           import os
    +           import torch_npu
    +           if os.environ.get('ROLLOUT_PROFILE', "false") == "true":
    +               # Initialize profiler
    +               import torch_npu
    +               experimental_config = torch_npu.profiler._ExperimentalConfig(
    +                   profiler_level=torch_npu.profiler.ProfilerLevel.Level1,
    +               )
    +               self.profiler_npu = torch_npu.profiler.profile(
    +                   activities=[torch_npu.profiler.ProfilerActivity.CPU, torch_npu.profiler.ProfilerActivity.NPU],
    +                   with_modules=os.environ.get('WITH_MODULES', "false") == "true",
    +                   profile_memory=os.environ.get('WITH_MEMORY', "false") == "true",
    +                   record_shapes=os.environ.get('WITH_SHAPE', "false") == "true",
    +                   with_stack=os.environ.get('WITH_STACK', "false") == "true",
    +                   experimental_config=experimental_config,
    +                   # Skip the first 29 steps, warmup 1 step, collect 30 steps, repeat 1 time.
    +                   schedule=torch_npu.profiler.schedule(wait=29, warmup=1, active=30, repeat=1),
    +                   on_trace_ready=torch_npu.profiler.tensorboard_trace_handler(os.environ.get('ROLLOUT_PROFILE_PATH'), analyse_flag=True)  # Data save path, whether to parse online
    +               )
    +               self.profiler_npu.start()

            def forward(self, forward_batch, **kwargs):
                # ... existing code ...

    +           import os
    +           if os.environ.get('ROLLOUT_PROFILE', "false") == "true":
    +               self.profiler_npu.step()  # Drive schedule to collect partial decode steps

                return output
<

*dev_guide-performance-ascend_profiling-fine-grained-collection-in-the-update-policy-actor-critic-phase*
3. Fine-grained Collection in the update_policy (Actor & Critic) Phase

The Update phase includes forward and backward propagation. In the unified
model engine, the mini-batch iteration is driven by
`TrainingWorker.train_mini_batch` in `verl/workers/engine_workers.py`, which
calls `train_batch` for each mini-batch.

FSDP Backend

The FSDP backend supports collection at both Mini-Batch and Micro-Batch
granularity. For Mini-Batch granularity, instrument
`TrainingWorker.train_mini_batch`; For Micro-Batch granularity, instrument
the micro-batch loop inside the FSDP engine's `forward_backward_batch`.

- Modified File: `verl/workers/engine_workers.py`
  (`TrainingWorker.train_mini_batch`, Mini-Batch granularity) or
  `verl/workers/engine/fsdp/transformer_impl.py`
  (`FSDPEngineWithLMHead.forward_backward_batch`, Micro-Batch granularity)

>diff
        class TrainingWorker(Worker, DistProfilerExtension):

            def __init__(self, config: TrainingWorkerConfig):
                # ...
    +           self.step = 1

            def train_mini_batch(self, data: TensorDict) -> TensorDict:
               # ...

    +          import os
    +          import torch_npu
    +          if self.step == int(os.environ.get('PROFILE_STEP', 1)) and os.environ.get('UPDATE_PROFILE', "false") == "true":
    +              # Prepare profiler
    +              experimental_config = torch_npu.profiler._ExperimentalConfig(
    +                  profiler_level=torch_npu.profiler.ProfilerLevel.Level1,
    +              )
    +              self.prof_npu = torch_npu.profiler.profile(
    +                  activities=[torch_npu.profiler.ProfilerActivity.CPU, torch_npu.profiler.ProfilerActivity.NPU],
    +                  with_modules=os.environ.get('WITH_MODULES', "false") == "true",
    +                  profile_memory=os.environ.get('WITH_MEMORY', "false") == "true",
    +                  record_shapes=os.environ.get('WITH_SHAPE', "false") == "true",
    +                  with_stack=os.environ.get('WITH_STACK', "false") == "true",
    +                  experimental_config=experimental_config,
    +                  # Only collect the first Mini Batch (including all Micro-Batch computations and one optimizer update)
    +                  schedule=torch_npu.profiler.schedule(wait=0, warmup=0, active=1, repeat=1),
    +                  on_trace_ready=torch_npu.profiler.tensorboard_trace_handler(os.environ.get('UPDATE_PROFILE_PATH'), analyse_flag=True)
    +              )
    +              if str(torch.distributed.get_rank()) in os.environ.get('PROFILE_RANKS', "0").split(','):
    +                  self.prof_npu.start()

               for batch_idx, mini_batch_td in enumerate(dataloader):
                   # ... internally calls self.train_batch(mini_batch_td), which in the engine
                   # runs Forward & Backward on each micro-batch and completes one optimizer update ...
                   actor_output = self.train_batch(mini_batch_td)

    +              if self.step == int(os.environ.get('PROFILE_STEP', 1)) and os.environ.get('UPDATE_PROFILE', "false") == "true":
    +                  # Drive schedule to collect mini batch; for micro-batch granularity, move self.prof_npu.step() into the micro_batch loop
    +                  if str(torch.distributed.get_rank()) in os.environ.get('PROFILE_RANKS', "0").split(','):
    +                      self.prof_npu.step()
    +          # This mini batch ends
    +          self.step += 1
<
Megatron Backend

The Megatron backend supports collection at Mini-Batch granularity, with the
same entry point `TrainingWorker.train_mini_batch`: The Megatron engine
internally runs Megatron's pipeline-parallel forward/backward schedule and
one optimizer step.

- Modified File: `verl/workers/engine_workers.py`
  (`TrainingWorker.train_mini_batch`) -- identical to the FSDP snippet
  above; it is recommended to rename the output directory (for example
  `./outputs/megatron_actor_update_profile`) to distinguish traces from
  different backends.

*dev_guide-performance-ascend_profiling-fine-grained-collection-in-the-compute-log-prob-actor-ref-phase*
4. Fine-grained Collection in the compute_log_prob (Actor & Ref) Phase

This phase computes the probability distributions of the new and old
policies. In the unified model engine, both actor and ref log-prob
computation goes through `TrainingWorker.infer_batch`, which dispatches to
the corresponding backend engine's `BaseEngine.infer_batch`.

FSDP Backend

The FSDP backend allows fine-grained control at the Micro-Batch level.
Instrument the micro-batch loop inside the FSDP engine forward pass.

- Modified File: `verl/workers/engine/fsdp/transformer_impl.py`
  (`FSDPEngineWithLMHead.forward_backward_batch` / `forward_step`)

>diff
        # ... import dependencies ...
    +   import torch_npu

        class FSDPEngineWithLMHead(FSDPEngine):

            def forward_backward_batch(self, data: TensorDict, loss_function, forward_only=False):

    +           role = "Ref" if forward_only and not self.optimizer_config else "Actor"
    +           # Prepare profiler (same configuration as above, omitted)
    +           experimental_config = torch_npu.profiler._ExperimentalConfig(...)
    +           self.prof_npu = torch_npu.profiler.profile(
    +               # ... (same configuration as above, omitted)
    +               # wait=0, warmup=0, active=1: directly collect the first micro-batch
    +               schedule=torch_npu.profiler.schedule(wait=0, warmup=0, active=1, repeat=1),
    +               on_trace_ready=torch_npu.profiler.tensorboard_trace_handler(f"./outputs/{role}_compute_log_prob", analyse_flag=True)
    +           )

    +           # forward_backward_batch is shared by ref and actor; use the role flag to distinguish;
    +           # To collect actor_compute_log_prob, switch to role == "Actor":
    +           if role == "Ref":
    +               self.prof_npu.start()

                for micro_batch in micro_batches:

                    # ... original computation logic ...
                    with torch.no_grad():
                        output = self.forward_step(micro_batch, loss_function, forward_only=True)

    +                   # Drive schedule to collect micro batch
    +                   if role == "Ref":
    +                       self.prof_npu.step()

                    # ...
<
Megatron Backend

Micro-Batch scheduling in the Megatron backend is managed internally by
Megatron's pipeline-parallel `forward_backward_func` and does not currently
support fine-grained collection at the Micro-Batch level through simple code
instrumentation. It is recommended to use the global profiler configuration
for collection.

----------------------------------------------------------------------------
                            *verl-dev_guide-performance-perf_tuning_on_ascend*
Ascend Performance Tuning Guide

Last updated: 01/29/2026.

Author: Xiaobo Hu https://github.com/tardis-key, Haozhe Li
https://github.com/ZLiao097

The performance tuning methods introduced in Perf Tuning
https://github.com/verl-project/verl/blob/main/docs/perf/perf_tuning.rst
also apply to Ascend devices. This document focuses on several
Ascend-specific tuning approaches, including fusion operator optimization,
specific hardware configurations, and Ascend affinity features.

                *dev_guide-performance-perf_tuning_on_ascend-fusion-operators*
FUSION OPERATORS

*dev_guide-performance-perf_tuning_on_ascend-list-of-commonly-used-fusion-operators*
List of Commonly Used Fusion Operators

The optimization principle of fusion operators is to use mathematically
equivalent substitutions to fuse the computation of multiple operators into
a single operator, reducing redundant computation and the number of
dispatches, thereby improving performance. Several typical NPU fusion
operators are listed below; all of them have already been substituted for
the Qwen2 and Qwen3 series models in npu_patch.py.

For the full set of fusion operators currently used in verl, refer to
npu_patch.py
https://github.com/verl-project/verl/blob/main/verl/models/transformers/npu_patch.py

*dev_guide-performance-perf_tuning_on_ascend-matrix-computation-communication-operator-fusion-mc2*
Matrix Computation-Communication operator fusion (MC2)

MC2 is a general term for a series of compute-communication fusion operators
in CANN. These operators fuse the originally serial communication and
computation operations together, optimizing performance through internal
splitting and pipelined parallel execution.

In vllm-ascend, you can specify the environment variable:

>sh
    export VLLM_ASCEND_ENABLE_MATMUL_ALLREDUCE=1
<
This enables `torch_npu.npu_mm_all_reduce_base` in the `RowParallelLinear`
of forward computation, merging the separate `matmul` and `allreduce` into a
single fusion operator.

RotaryMul&RotaryMulGrad      *dev_guide-performance-perf_tuning_on_ascend-id1*

torch_npu interface: `torch_npu.npu_rotary_mul(x, r1, r2)`

Parameter description:

- x: q, k. The shape requires 4-dimensional input, generally `[B, N, S, D]`
  or `[B, S, N, D]` or `[S, B, N, D]`.
- r1: cos value. The shape requires 4-dimensional input, generally
  `[1, 1, S, D]` or `[1, S, 1, D]` or `[S, 1, 1, D]`.
- r2: sin value. The shape requires 4-dimensional input, generally
  `[1, 1, S, D]` or `[1, S, 1, D]` or `[S, 1, 1, D]`.

RmsNorm&RmsNormGrad          *dev_guide-performance-perf_tuning_on_ascend-id2*

torch_npu interface:
`torch_npu.npu_rms_norm(self, gamma, epsilon=1e-06) -> (Tensor, Tensor)`
Parameter description:

- self: Tensor type, shape supports 1-8 dimensions.
- gamma: Tensor type, usually the weight. The shape must match the last few
  dimensions of self.
- epsilon: Float data type, used to prevent divide-by-zero errors.

Output description:

- The 1st output is a Tensor, the final output y of the formula.
- The 2nd output is a Tensor, the intermediate result rstd of rms_norm, used
  for backward computation.

Swiglu                       *dev_guide-performance-perf_tuning_on_ascend-id3*

torch_npu interface:
`torch_npu.npu_swiglu(Tensor self, int dim=-1) -> (Tensor)`

Parameter description:

- self: Tensor type, shape supports 1-8 dimensions.
- dim: Int type, default is -1.

Output description:

- The output is a Tensor, the final output y of the formula.

GroupMatMul                  *dev_guide-performance-perf_tuning_on_ascend-id4*

Function prototype:

>python
    npu_grouped_matmul(
        x,
        weight,
        *,
        bias=None,
        scale=None,
        offset=None,
        antiquant_scale=None,
        antiquant_offset=None,
        per_token_scale=None,
        group_list=None,
        activation_input=None,
        activation_quant_scale=None,
        activation_quant_offset=None,
        split_item=0, group_type=None,
        group_list_type=0,
        act_type=0,
        output_dtype=None,
        tuning_config=None
    ) -> List[Tensor]
<
For detailed usage, refer to the linked title documentation.

*dev_guide-performance-perf_tuning_on_ascend-using-fusion-operators-with-the-fsdp-backend*
Using Fusion Operators with the FSDP Backend

In `verl/models/transformers/npu_patch.py`, the available fusion operators
have already been substituted through patching. No additional operations are
required; they are used by default.

*dev_guide-performance-perf_tuning_on_ascend-using-fusion-operators-with-the-megatron-backend*
Using Fusion Operators with the Megatron Backend

Megatron's fusion operators are integrated in MindSpeed and require specific
parameters to enable:

1. Flash Attention (must be enabled)

   >text
       +actor_rollout_ref.actor.megatron.override_transformer_config.use_flash_attn=True
<

2. RotaryMul

   >text
       +actor_rollout_ref.actor.megatron.override_transformer_config.apply_rope_fusion=True
       +actor_rollout_ref.actor.megatron.override_transformer_config.use_fused_rotary_pos_emb=True
<

3. RMSNorm

   >text
       +actor_rollout_ref.actor.megatron.override_transformer_config.use_fused_rmsnorm=True
<

4. GroupMatMul

   >text
       +actor_rollout_ref.actor.megatron.override_transformer_config.moe_grouped_gemm=True
<

5. Swiglu

   >text
       +actor_rollout_ref.actor.megatron.override_transformer_config.use_fused_swiglu=True
<

6. Permute/Unpermute

   >text
       +actor_rollout_ref.actor.megatron.override_transformer_config.fused_permute_unpermute=True
<

7. MC2

   >text
       +actor_rollout_ref.actor.megatron.override_transformer_config.use_ascend_mc2=True
<

    *dev_guide-performance-perf_tuning_on_ascend-ascend-general-configuration*
ASCEND GENERAL CONFIGURATION

Operator Dispatch            *dev_guide-performance-perf_tuning_on_ascend-id5*

`TASK_QUEUE_ENABLE` configures the optimization level of the task_queue
operator dispatch queue, defaulting to Level 1. This configuration can
reduce host dispatch time and can be used to alleviate the issue of
excessive overall free time caused by dispatch.

Level 0: Dispatch pipeline optimization is not enabled.

Level 1: Splits operator dispatch tasks into two stages. Some tasks (mainly
aclnn operator calls) are placed on the newly added secondary pipeline. The
primary and secondary pipelines pass tasks to each other through the
operator queue and run in parallel. Through partial masking, the overall
dispatch time is reduced and end-to-end performance is improved.

Level 2: Further balances the task load between the primary and secondary
pipelines based on Level 1, mainly by migrating workspace-related tasks to
the secondary pipeline. The masking effect is better and the performance
gain is greater. This configuration takes effect only in binary scenarios;
the recommended value is Level 2.

                             *dev_guide-performance-perf_tuning_on_ascend-id6*
Communication Algorithm Orchestration Expansion

Use the environment variable `HCCL_OP_EXPANSION_MODE=AIV` to configure the
orchestration expansion location of the communication algorithm. Supported
values are as follows:

- AI_CPU: The orchestration expansion location of the communication
  algorithm is the AI CPU on the Device side. The Device side automatically
  selects the corresponding scheduler based on the hardware model.
- AIV: The orchestration expansion location of the communication algorithm
  is the Vector Core on the Device side, and execution is also on the Vector
  Core.
- HOST: The orchestration expansion location of the communication algorithm
  is the Host-side CPU. The Device side automatically selects the
  corresponding scheduler based on the hardware model.
- HOST_TS: The orchestration expansion location of the communication
  algorithm is the Host-side CPU. The Host dispatches tasks to the Device's
  Task Scheduler, and the Device's Task Scheduler performs task scheduling
  and execution.

          *dev_guide-performance-perf_tuning_on_ascend-inference-phase-tuning*
INFERENCE PHASE TUNING

           *dev_guide-performance-perf_tuning_on_ascend-chunked-prefill-in-v1*
Chunked Prefill in V1

The current version of vLLM has VLLM V1 enabled by default. Use the
following configuration to enable Chunked Prefill:

>python
    actor_rollout_ref.rollout.enable_chunked_prefill=True
<
For the principle, refer to the vLLM official documentation
https://docs.vllm.ai/en/v0.4.2/models/performance.html.

Graph Mode            *dev_guide-performance-perf_tuning_on_ascend-graph-mode*

Similar to CUDA, the NPU enables ACL Graph through the following
configuration:

>python
    actor_rollout_ref.rollout.enforce_eager=False
<
  Note: ~

  ACL Graph conflicts in principle with `taskqueue Level 2`; the two cannot
  be enabled at the same time.

           *dev_guide-performance-perf_tuning_on_ascend-training-phase-tuning*
TRAINING PHASE TUNING

FSDP                        *dev_guide-performance-perf_tuning_on_ascend-fsdp*

FSDP                               Description
---------------------------------  -------------------------------------------
/                                  Shard optimizer only (Zero-1)
SHARD_GRAD_OP                      Shard gradients and optimizer (Zero-2)
HYBRID_SHARD                       Shard weights, gradients, and optimizer
                                   (Zero-3)
2D device_mesh+HYBRID_SHARD        Also known as HSDP (FSDP+DDP). For example,
                                   device_mesh=[2,8]: every 8 ranks form one
                                   FSDP group, with FSDP sharding inside the
                                   group. There are two groups that perform
                                   DDP between them, synchronizing gradients
                                   through allreduce.
2D device_mesh+HYBRID_SHARD_ZERO2  The Zero-2 version of HSDP
NO_SHARD                           DDP

FSDP does not support Zero-1. VeRL determines the device mesh value based on
the number of cards and `actor_rollout_ref.actor.fsdp_config.fsdp_size`, and
uses Zero-3 for sharding by default. If the model is small (recommended to
be smaller than 7B), you can set
`actor_rollout_ref.actor.fsdp_config.reshard_after_forward` to `True` to use
Zero-2 on FSDP/FSDP2 to optimize performance.

Megatron                *dev_guide-performance-perf_tuning_on_ascend-megatron*

When the model is large, using Megatron as the training backend allows for
more flexible performance tuning.

When DP parallelism cannot fit the model in memory, enable TP first to shard
the model weights. If the model is still too large, enable PP for further
sharding. If the sequence is too long, causing the activations to be too
large, you can enable CP and SP for optimization. In MoE models, you can
additionally enable EP to control expert sharding. If the experts are too
small, to avoid sharding the weights too finely, you can enable ETP to avoid
TP sharding in the MoE part and instead distribute multiple complete experts
across DP and TP.

TP, PP, EP, and ETP are used in the same way as Megatron. The way to enable
CP and SP on the NPU is as follows:

- SP: `Sequence Parallel` further improves computation efficiency on top of
  Tensor Parallel. It is a parallel computation method that shards the input
  data along the sequence dimension. On the NPU, SP is invoked through
  MindSpeed:

  >python
      actor_rollout_ref.actor.megatron.override_transformer_config.sequence_parallel=True
<

- CP: `Context Parallel` is a method for parallel processing of neural
  network activations across multiple GPUs/NPUs. It is implemented by
  partitioning the input tensor along the sequence dimension. On the NPU, CP
  is invoked through MindSpeed (the two parameters must be added together):

  >text
      actor_rollout_ref.actor.megatron.context_parallel_size
      actor_rollout_ref.actor.megatron.override_transformer_config.context_parallel_size
<

  *dev_guide-performance-perf_tuning_on_ascend-megatron-distributed-optimizer*
Megatron-distributed optimizer

When facing larger-sized models, it is usually necessary to shard the
optimizer to each card within a DP domain to save memory. To enable the
distributed optimizer on the NPU under the Megatron backend:

>text
    +actor_rollout_ref.actor.megatron.override_transformer_config.use_distributed_optimizer=True
<

----------------------------------------------------------------------------
                       *verl-dev_guide-precision_analysis-precision_alignment*
Precision Alignment Guide

When performing reinforcement learning (RL) training in the VeRL framework,
precision alignment is a key step to ensure that the training process is
reproducible and debuggable.

This document summarizes the methods for precision alignment between NPU and
GPU on VeRL for reference.

Last updated: 05/09/2026.

*dev_guide-precision_analysis-precision_alignment-environment-and-weight-alignment*
1. ENVIRONMENT AND WEIGHT ALIGNMENT

*dev_guide-precision_analysis-precision_alignment-dependency-version-alignment*
1.1 Dependency Version Alignment

The versions of VeRL and transformers must be strictly aligned; otherwise,
the precision results will be directly affected.

For other key dependencies (torch, megatron, vllm), if strict alignment is
not possible, priority should be given to keeping them consistent or close.

     *dev_guide-precision_analysis-precision_alignment-model-weight-alignment*
1.2 Model Weight Alignment

Check whether the model weights and the config.json file are completely
identical.

       *dev_guide-precision_analysis-precision_alignment-input-data-alignment*
2. INPUT DATA ALIGNMENT

Add the following configuration to the verl training startup script:

>python
    data.shuffle=False
    data.validation_shuffle=False
<

    *dev_guide-precision_analysis-precision_alignment-configuration-alignment*
3. CONFIGURATION ALIGNMENT

When performing precision alignment between NPU and GPU, you need to check
whether the configurations are fully aligned. This includes:

1. Directly compare the configurations written in the scripts.
2. Save logs during the run, collect the configurations printed in the logs
   for comparison. You can compare whether the default parameter
   configurations are consistent, and you must ensure that key parameters
   are aligned.

         *dev_guide-precision_analysis-precision_alignment-fixing-determinism*
4. FIXING DETERMINISM

        *dev_guide-precision_analysis-precision_alignment-fixing-random-seeds*
4.1 Fixing Random Seeds

Install `msprobe` in the environment:

>bash
    pip install mindstudio-probe
<
Add the determinism function at the beginning of the worker file:

>python
    from msprobe.pytorch import seed_all
    seed_all(mode=True)
<

*dev_guide-precision_analysis-precision_alignment-fixing-communication-environment-variables*
4.2 Fixing Communication Environment Variables

In multi-card communication scenarios:

- Under HCCL communication (default scenario):

  - export CLOSE_MATMUL_K_SHIFT=1
  - export ATB_MATMUL_SHUFFLE_K_ENABLE=0
  - export HCCL_DETERMINISTIC=”true”
  - export VLLM_ENABLE_V1_MULTIPROCESSING=0

- Under LCCL communication (enabled through export
  HCCL_OP_EXPANSION_MODE=”AIV”):

  - export CLOSE_MATMUL_K_SHIFT=1
  - export ATB_MATMUL_SHUFFLE_K_ENABLE=0
  - export LCCL_DETERMINISTIC=1
  - export ATB_LLM_LCOC_ENABLE=0
  - export VLLM_ENABLE_V1_MULTIPROCESSING=0

In single-card no-communication scenarios:

- export CLOSE_MATMUL_K_SHIFT=1
- export ATB_MATMUL_SHUFFLE_K_ENABLE=0
- export VLLM_ENABLE_V1_MULTIPROCESSING=0

*dev_guide-precision_analysis-precision_alignment-verifying-training-precision*
5. VERIFYING TRAINING PRECISION

*dev_guide-precision_analysis-precision_alignment-training-instrumentation-stubbing*
5.1 Training Instrumentation (Stubbing)

Instrumentation (stubbing) means preserving the input and output data of the
current stage to facilitate comparative analysis of the results. When
troubleshooting precision issues, instrumentation is needed to help locate
the problem. A common instrumentation approach is to directly dump the data
from the rollout phase.

Step 1: Generate baseline data on the GPU environment

Run the GPU script once with the following configuration enabled:

>python
    skip.rollout_tq.enable=True \
    skip.rollout_tq.dump_dir=/path/to/rollout_dump \
    'skip.rollout_tq.steps=[1,2,3]' \
    skip.rollout_tq.action=cache
<
This saves the TransferQueue trajectory cache for steps 1--3. Copy the cache
directory to the NPU environment at the same path, keeping the experiment
name, project name, and batch settings unchanged. JSONL rollout logs cannot
be used as this cache.

Step 2: Reproduce and verify on the NPU environment

Enable the following parameters on the NPU, reuse the sequence generated in
the previous step, and run end-to-end:

>python
    skip.rollout_tq.enable=True \
    skip.rollout_tq.dump_dir=/path/to/rollout_dump \
    'skip.rollout_tq.steps=[1,2,3]' \
    skip.rollout_tq.action=cache
<
Step 3: Compare metrics

With the same stubbed inference input, consistent training configuration,
and fixed randomness, compare whether there are differences in the
rewards/pg_loss/grad_norm values between NPU and GPU.

*dev_guide-precision_analysis-precision_alignment-verifying-inference-precision*
6. VERIFYING INFERENCE PRECISION

6.1 resharding   *dev_guide-precision_analysis-precision_alignment-resharding*

Before inference officially starts, vllm performs a dummy run that infers
one token to estimate the memory usage during inference and then allocates
memory. You can specify the load_format parameter during vLLM's LLM
initialization to determine whether the dummy run uses randomly initialized
weights (dummy) or real weights (safetensors). In VeRL, this parameter is
specified through actor_rollout_ref.rollout.load_format.

When garbled inference output occurs, if the engine initialization uses
load_format=dummy, then the sharding is highly likely to have a problem.
Even if the output becomes normal after switching to safetensors, the
sharding still has issues and the forward pass needs to be compared.

 *dev_guide-precision_analysis-precision_alignment-inference-result-alignment*
6.2 Inference Result Alignment

>python
    trainer.rollout_data_dir=/path/to/rollout_dump
<
Save the inference result of each step as a jsonl file. You can directly
open the jsonl file to quickly confirm whether the whole-network inference
result is garbled, which is used to scope inference precision issues.

Before dumping inference data, if reproducing the inference precision issue
requires a lot of resources, you can first try to reduce the cost of
reproducing the inference precision issue by reducing the reproduction scale
and the amount of data that needs to be dumped and compared. In multi-batch
and long-sequence scenarios, you can try to reproduce by sending
single-batch requests and reducing the sequence length.

            *dev_guide-precision_analysis-precision_alignment-dump-comparison*
7. DUMP COMPARISON

|verl-dev_guide-precision_analysis-precision_debugger|. After locating the
stage where the problem occurs, you can use the msprobe tool to dump data
for detailed localization.

During inference or training, the model may produce outputs that deviate
from expectations, generate anomalies, or even produce numerical instability
issues such as NaN/Inf. To locate the root cause, you need to finely monitor
the model execution path, collect intermediate features, weights, activation
values, and the inputs and outputs of each key layer, and record context
information such as prompts, tensor dtype, and hardware configuration. By
capturing these core tensors and metadata, you can systematically trace the
source of precision degradation or numerical errors.

----------------------------------------------------------------------------
                        *verl-dev_guide-precision_analysis-precision_debugger*
Precision Debugger (msprobe) in verl

Last updated: 04/13/2026.

This guide explains how to collect precision data in verl using the
`msprobe` PrecisionDebugger.

PREREQUISITES  *dev_guide-precision_analysis-precision_debugger-prerequisites*

- Install `msprobe` in the training environment:

>bash
    pip install mindstudio-probe
<
- Prepare a `config.json` for msprobe (see examples below).
- Enable profiler for the roles you want to collect.

Reference:

- `https://gitcode.com/Ascend/msprobe.git`

CONFIGURATION  *dev_guide-precision_analysis-precision_debugger-configuration*

PrecisionDebugger is integrated through verl's unified profiler interface.
Use a minimal two-part setup:

- `global_profiler` selects the tool and config file.
- role `profiler.enable=True` turns on profiling for that role.

    *dev_guide-precision_analysis-precision_debugger-global-profiling-control*
Global profiling control

In `global_profiler`, set the profiler tool to `precision_debugger` and
configure the msprobe-specific options under `global_tool_config`.

>yaml
    global_profiler:
      tool: precision_debugger
      steps: [1, 2, 5]
      save_path: "outputs/profile"
      global_tool_config:
        precision_debugger:
          _target_: verl.utils.profiler.config.PrecisionDebuggerToolConfig
          config_path: /path/to/config.json
          stages:
            - actor_update
            - actor_compute_log_prob
            - ref_compute_log_prob
            - compute_values
            - critic_update
            - compute_rm_score
          strict: False
<
Notes:

- `global_profiler.steps` is the only step filter for PrecisionDebugger.
- Dumps are written under `global_profiler.save_path`.
- Actual dump path is
  `{global_profiler.save_path}/step_{global_step}/{stage}`.
- Do not set `dump_path` in `config.json`; output path is controlled by
  verl.

      *dev_guide-precision_analysis-precision_debugger-role-profiling-control*
Role profiling control

Enable profiling for the roles you want to collect:

>yaml
    actor_rollout_ref:
      actor:
        profiler:
          enable: True
      ref:
        profiler:
          enable: True
    critic:
      profiler:
        enable: True
<

            *dev_guide-precision_analysis-precision_debugger-supported-stages*
SUPPORTED STAGES

PrecisionDebugger collects data from the following stages:

- `actor_update`
- `actor_compute_log_prob`
- `ref_compute_log_prob`
- `compute_values`
- `critic_update`
- `compute_rm_score`

Rollout generation is intentionally skipped (`rollout_generate` is ignored).

The current integration is designed for training-side stages. In a typical
PPO run, the most common useful combinations are:

- actor/ref only: `actor_compute_log_prob`, `ref_compute_log_prob`,
  `actor_update`
- actor/ref/critic: `actor_compute_log_prob`, `ref_compute_log_prob`,
  `compute_values`, `critic_update`, `actor_update`

*dev_guide-precision_analysis-precision_debugger-msprobe-config-json-common-examples*
MSPROBE CONFIG.JSON COMMON EXAMPLES

             *dev_guide-precision_analysis-precision_debugger-statistics-mode*
`statistics` mode

>json
    {
      "task": "statistics",
      "rank": [],
      "step": [],
      "level": "L1",
      "async_dump": false,
      "statistics": {
        "scope": [],
        "list": [],
        "tensor_list": [],
        "data_mode": ["all"],
        "summary_mode": "statistics"
      }
    }
<

`tensor` mode    *dev_guide-precision_analysis-precision_debugger-tensor-mode*

>json
    {
      "task": "tensor",
      "rank": [],
      "step": [],
      "level": "L1",
      "async_dump": false,
      "tensor": {
        "scope": [],
        "list": [],
        "data_mode": ["all"],
        "summary_mode": "statistics"
      }
    }
<

             *dev_guide-precision_analysis-precision_debugger-minimal-example*
MINIMAL EXAMPLE

The following example enables PrecisionDebugger on steps `1` and `2`. If you
need rank filtering, configure it only in msprobe `config.json`.

>yaml
    global_profiler:
      tool: precision_debugger
      steps: [1, 2]
      global_tool_config:
        precision_debugger:
          _target_: verl.utils.profiler.config.PrecisionDebuggerToolConfig
          config_path: /path/to/dump_config.json
          stages:
            - actor_compute_log_prob
            - ref_compute_log_prob
            - actor_update
          strict: False

    actor_rollout_ref:
      actor:
        profiler:
          enable: True
      ref:
        profiler:
          enable: True
<

         *dev_guide-precision_analysis-precision_debugger-minimal-cli-example*
MINIMAL CLI EXAMPLE

Use only the required flags:

>bash
    python3 -m verl.trainer.main_ppo \
      global_profiler.tool=precision_debugger \
      global_profiler.steps='[1,2]' \
      global_profiler.save_path=outputs/profile \
      +global_profiler.global_tool_config.precision_debugger.config_path=/path/to/config.json \
      actor_rollout_ref.actor.profiler.enable=True \
      actor_rollout_ref.ref.profiler.enable=True
<
Optional stage filter:

>bash
    +global_profiler.global_tool_config.precision_debugger.stages='[actor_compute_log_prob,ref_compute_log_prob,actor_update]'
<

OUTPUT LAYOUT  *dev_guide-precision_analysis-precision_debugger-output-layout*

Verl organizes PrecisionDebugger output by training global step and stage.
Inside each stage directory, msprobe creates its own `step*/rank*` layout.

Example:

>text
    outputs/profile/
      step_1/
        actor_compute_log_prob/step0/rank0/dump.json
        actor_update/step0/rank0/dump.json
        ref_compute_log_prob/step0/rank0/dump.json
      step_2/
        actor_compute_log_prob/step0/rank0/dump.json
        actor_update/step0/rank0/dump.json
        ref_compute_log_prob/step0/rank0/dump.json
<
Observed output from a real run:

- Outer `step_<global_step>` directories are created by verl.
- Inner `step0/rank0/` directories and `dump.json` files are created by
  msprobe.
- With the current integration, each profiled stage is collected in an
  independent dump session, so stage-local output typically lands in
  `step0`.

     *dev_guide-precision_analysis-precision_debugger-how-results-are-written*
HOW RESULTS ARE WRITTEN

The verl integration wraps each profiled stage with:

- `debugger.start(model=...)`
- execute the stage
- `debugger.stop()`
- `service.reset_status()` if the msprobe runtime exposes it

Verl does not manually call `debugger.step()` in the current integration.
Instead, each stage writes to its own dump directory and resets msprobe
runtime status after `stop()` to avoid stale `dump.json` cache growth across
stages.

For L0 collection, PrecisionDebugger must bind to the actual model used in
the stage. The profiler resolves the model inside
`verl/utils/profiler/precision_debugger_profile.py` and supports both legacy
workers and the newer model-engine worker path.

     *dev_guide-precision_analysis-precision_debugger-overhead-and-disk-usage*
OVERHEAD AND DISK USAGE

Below are measurements from a real PPO run on Ascend with:

- model: `Qwen2-0.5B`
- profiled steps: `[1, 2]`
- rank: `0`
- stages:

  - L1: `actor_compute_log_prob`, `ref_compute_log_prob`, `actor_update`
  - L0: `actor_compute_log_prob`, `ref_compute_log_prob`, `compute_values`,
    `critic_update`, `actor_update`

Time overhead  *dev_guide-precision_analysis-precision_debugger-time-overhead*

Run       Model       Profiled steps            Measured step time
--------  ----------  ------------------------  ------------------------------
Baseline  Qwen2-0.5B  None                      about 16-18 s/step in steady
                                                state
L0        Qwen2-0.5B  step 1                    66.81 s
L0        Qwen2-0.5B  step 2                    48.78 s
L0        Qwen2-0.5B  non-profiled later steps  about 17 s/step
L1        Qwen2-0.5B  step 1                    177.35 s
L1        Qwen2-0.5B  step 2                    161.80 s
L1        Qwen2-0.5B  non-profiled later steps  about 17 s/step

In this experiment, profiled L0 steps were about `3x-4x` slower than the
baseline steady-state step time, and profiled L1 steps were about `9x-10x`
slower. Non-profiled later steps remained close to baseline in both cases.

In general, PrecisionDebugger should be treated as a heavy-weight precision
debugging tool rather than a lightweight profiler. In larger models or
broader stage coverage, it is common to observe `tens-X` performance
inflation for profiled steps.

Disk usage        *dev_guide-precision_analysis-precision_debugger-disk-usage*

Level  Model       Stages                   Scope             Disk usage
-----  ----------  -----------------------  ----------------  ----------------
L1     Qwen2-0.5B  actor_compute_log_prob,  total for step_1  21 MB
                   ref_compute_log_prob,    and step_2
                   actor_update
L1     Qwen2-0.5B  actor_compute_log_prob,  per step          about 11 MB
                   ref_compute_log_prob,
                   actor_update
L1     Qwen2-0.5B  actor_update             per step          about 5.1-5.2 MB
L1     Qwen2-0.5B  actor_compute_log_prob   per step          about 2.6 MB
L1     Qwen2-0.5B  ref_compute_log_prob     per step          about 2.6 MB
L0     Qwen2-0.5B  actor_compute_log_prob,  total for step_1  8.8 MB
                   ref_compute_log_prob,    and step_2
                   actor_update
L0     Qwen2-0.5B  actor_compute_log_prob,  per step          about 4.4 MB
                   ref_compute_log_prob,
                   actor_update
L0     Qwen2-0.5B  actor_update             per step          about 2.5 MB
L0     Qwen2-0.5B  actor_compute_log_prob   per step          about 1.1 MB
L0     Qwen2-0.5B  ref_compute_log_prob     per step          about 0.86-0.87
                                                              MB

In this experiment, total L1 disk usage was about `2.4x` the L0 disk usage
for the measured actor/ref stage set.

These numbers depend on:

- selected stages
- number of profiled steps
- dump level and task
- model shape and sequence length

      *dev_guide-precision_analysis-precision_debugger-how-to-analyze-results*
HOW TO ANALYZE RESULTS

At minimum, check:

- which `step_<global_step>` directory was generated
- which stage directories exist under that step
- whether `dump.json` exists under `step0/rank0`

For downstream analysis, use standard msprobe tools such as:

- `msprobe compare`
- `msprobe visualization`

Example compare usage:

>bash
    msprobe compare \
      --target-path /path/to/target_dump/dump.json \
      --golden-path /path/to/golden_dump/dump.json
<
You can compare:

- the same stage across two runs
- different global steps of the same stage
- different ranks when multi-rank collection is enabled

For more advanced analysis workflows, refer to the official msprobe
documentation for compare and visualization commands.

USAGE NOTES      *dev_guide-precision_analysis-precision_debugger-usage-notes*

- Verl integrates PrecisionDebugger through `DistProfiler.annotate` wrappers
  on training stages.
- PrecisionDebugger is automatically discrete: each profiled stage is
  collected in an independent `start -> stop -> reset_status` session. It
  does not currently expose the unified profiler `discrete` configuration
  used by tools such as `nsys` or `npu`.
- `global_steps` is read from batch `meta_info` or from worker attributes.
- If `strict` is `True`, missing msprobe or unknown stages raise errors.
- If a stage prints `PrecisionDebugger model not resolved`, that stage ran
  normally but no dump was collected because verl could not bind msprobe to
  a valid model object.
- Because dump cost is high, prefer collecting a small number of
  representative steps first, then narrow the stage set if necessary.

           *dev_guide-precision_analysis-precision_debugger-quality-checklist*
QUALITY CHECKLIST

Use this checklist to verify your setup is complete and reproducible:

- `global_profiler.tool=precision_debugger`
- `global_profiler.steps` includes the target step
- `+global_profiler.global_tool_config.precision_debugger.config_path=...`
  is set
- role `profiler.enable=True` is set for the stages you need
- `msprobe` is importable in the runtime environment
- output exists under
  `{global_profiler.save_path}/step_<global_step>/<stage>/...`

             *dev_guide-precision_analysis-precision_debugger-troubleshooting*
TROUBLESHOOTING

*dev_guide-precision_analysis-precision_debugger-no-dump-directory-is-generated*
No dump directory is generated

Check:

- `global_profiler.tool=precision_debugger`
- `global_profiler.steps` contains the target step
- role profiler is enabled for the target role
- msprobe is installed in the training environment

*dev_guide-precision_analysis-precision_debugger-precisiondebugger-model-not-resolved*
`PrecisionDebugger model not resolved`

This means the stage was reached, but verl could not find the actual model
used by that worker. The stage itself still runs, but dump is skipped. This
usually indicates:

- a new worker path was introduced and profiler model resolution needs to be
  updated
- the role or engine backend differs from the paths currently supported by
  the resolver

*dev_guide-precision_analysis-precision_debugger-dump-json-keeps-growing-unexpectedly*
`dump.json` keeps growing unexpectedly

If `stop()` is called without resetting msprobe runtime state, cached dump
data may continue to accumulate across stage invocations. The current verl
integration resets msprobe runtime status after `stop()` when the service
API supports it.
